{\raggedright\LARGE\sffamily\bfseries AST-Route: An AST-Aware Multi-Objective Query Routing and Cost-Prediction Framework Across Serverless Cloud, \mbox{Vectorized} CPU (C++/Rust), and GPU-Accelerated OLAP Engines}\\[0.9em]

{\large\textbf{Preecha Noiumkar\textsuperscript{1,*}}}\\[4pt]
\textsuperscript{1}Department of Information Technology, Faculty of Informatics, Mahasarakham University, Kham Riang, \mbox{Kantharawichai}, Maha Sarakham 44150, Thailand.\\
\textsuperscript{*}Correspondence: Preecha Noiumkar (\href{mailto:preecha.n@msu.ac.th}{preecha.n@msu.ac.th})\\[0.8em]

\noindent\rule{\textwidth}{0.8pt}
\begin{quote}
\small\sffamily
\textbf{\color{springerblue}Abstract}\\[0.3em]
\textbf{Background:} Analytical SQL workloads span vectorized C++/Rust CPUs, discrete GPUs, and serverless cloud warehouses across memory-wall regimes ($\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$). Static assignment incurs PCIe transfer stalls on small scans, host memory spilling on large joins, or cloud scheduling overhead, while unconstrained point-estimate routing triggers severe tail-latency regret near hardware crossovers.

\textbf{Methodology:} We present \textbf{AST-Route}, a pre-execution SQL router for tail-risk-controlled dispatch across six OLAP backends: SQLite~3 ($0\%$ share) and five active candidates (DuckDB, Polars, Apache DataFusion, Google BigQuery, and an author-developed PyTorch CUDA tensor prototype). Without physical plan compilation, AST-Route extracts a 9D SQL Abstract Syntax Tree and zone-map vector ($\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$, extended to $\mathbb{R}^{10}$ across scales and $\mathbb{R}^{12}$ with $10.2\text{ KB}$ catalog sketches) under three overhead regimes ($4.15\text{ ms}$ cold SQL, $0.67\text{ ms}$ AST-template cache, $0.08\text{ ms}$ 1D threshold cache), governed by a PCIe margin guard (nested-CV modal $\hat{\tau}_{\text{guard}}^{(-k)}=8\%$; canonical $\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$) and cross-conformal gates.

\textbf{Results:} Across $\mathbf{213}$ benchmark queries from seven suites ($\mathbf{228}$ including $15$ Seed~\texttt{S6} holdouts), AST-Route under $25$-seed Leave-One-Seed-Out CV ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) achieves $\mathbf{55.37\text{ ms}}$ net mean latency under nested-CV ($\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ canonical)---establishing $\pm 5.0\text{ ms}$ TOST equivalence ($p_{\text{TOST}}=0.0442$ nested-CV) and non-inferiority ($p_{\text{non-inf}}=0.00067$ canonical) to Always GPU ($56.96\text{ ms}$) while cutting P95 tail regret by $\mathbf{2.17\times\text{--}2.25\times}$ ($\mathbf{118.40\%}$ nested-CV and $\mathbf{114.11\%}$ canonical vs.\ $\mathbf{257.31\%}$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0196$) and beating static CPUs by $1.50\times\text{--}3.35\times$ ($p_{\text{Holm,seed}}\le 0.0171$). Pure native-SQL routing ($69.12\text{ ms}$) captures $\mathbf{48.5\%}$ of the six-engine routed gain ($\mathbf{82.76\%}$ Top-1, $\mathbf{98.28\%}$ Top-2 across $116$ external queries), while a minimal two-engine subset (Polars or DuckDB + GPU) captures $\mathbf{97.3\%\text{--}98.7\%}$ of the routed gain and Pareto policies cut energy by $\mathbf{24.3\%\text{--}41.2\%}$ or save $\mathbf{\$507.06/1\text{M}}$ at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$.

\textbf{Conclusion:} AST-Route enables tail-safe cross-engine orchestration with explicit safeguards for unseen operators and skewed string predicates.

\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} Cross-engine query routing; Tail-risk control; Conformal prediction; Heterogeneous OLAP systems; Apache Arrow and GPU analytics; Multi-objective cost modeling
\end{quote}

\section{Introduction}
Modern enterprise analytics increasingly operate over heterogeneous, polyglot data infrastructures spanning four distinct computing paradigms: elastic serverless cloud data warehouses such as Google BigQuery \cite{melnik2010dremel, melnik2020dremel, lounissi2025funda}, in-process C++ vectorized analytical database systems such as DuckDB \cite{raasveldt2019duckdb, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc}, Rust-based Apache Arrow streaming query engines such as Polars and Apache DataFusion \cite{lamb2024datafusion}, and SIMT GPU-accelerated relational engines \cite{chrysogelos2019hetexchange, shanbhag2020crystal, bress2013hype}, alongside distributed multi-node systems like ClickHouse \cite{schulze2024clickhouse} and Trino \cite{sethi2019presto}. While cloud-native disaggregated storage-compute architectures provide massive horizontal slot concurrency for petabyte-scale workloads \cite{shojaee2024serverless, abdalla2022bigdata}, they introduce non-trivial stateless scheduling and distributed shuffle overheads on moderate-scale datasets \cite{begoli2018calcite, garcia2008database, lounissi2025funda}. Concurrently, modern local hardware presents an intricate architectural trade-off between CPU SIMD vectorization over Apache Arrow memory buffers and discrete GPU offloading across the PCIe interconnect \cite{milicevic2024dlquery, root2019mapd, kim2022blazingsql}.

Because each execution paradigm exhibits fundamentally different algorithmic strengths and memory-hierarchy bottlenecks across the discrete scale factors defined by the Transaction Processing Performance Council ($\text{SF} \in \{1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}\}$ in the TPC-H specification \cite{tpc2022tpch, boncz2014tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}), committing an entire analytical workload to any single static engine results in severe performance regret, excessive energy consumption, out-of-core spilling penalties, or unnecessary cloud FinOps expenditure. Designing an automated, pre-execution query routing framework capable of dynamically dispatching SQL queries to their optimal execution backend requires overcoming three critical research gaps in the existing literature \cite{ahmed2020hibench, oneil2009ssb, leis2015job}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Research Gap 1 (Limitations of Single-Engine Learned Optimizers, Cloud Predictors, and Plan-Dependent Polystore Routers):} Existing learned query optimizers such as Neo \cite{marcus2019neo} and Bao \cite{marcus2021bao} steer physical operator plans \emph{within} a single database engine, Zero-Shot Cost Models \cite{hilprecht2022zeroshot} require physical execution plan graphs, cloud stage predictors \cite{ganapathi2009predicting, nathan2024stage} estimate runtimes within a single cloud warehouse, and cross-platform/polystore optimizers such as BigDAWG \cite{tan2023stage} and RHEEM / Apache Wayang \cite{gupta2023adaptive} require compiling query sub-plans into platform-specific operator DAGs using calibrated point-estimate cost functions without modeling Apache Arrow vs.\ PCIe GPU transfer thresholds or distribution-free conformal tail-risk bounds. A lightweight pre-execution framework that extracts static Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) features and zone-map selectivity directly from raw SQL to route queries across C++ DBMS, Rust Arrow, CUDA GPU, and serverless cloud paradigms remains underdeveloped.
    \item \textbf{Research Gap 2 (Unmodeled PCIe Crossover Thresholds, Memory Walls, and Device-Level Energy Costs):} Prior GPU database studies \cite{shanbhag2020crystal, root2019mapd, kim2022blazingsql} predominantly report warm in-VRAM kernel speedups at fixed small scale factors ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) while omitting cold-start PCIe Host-to-Device (H2D) transfer overheads, chunked PCIe streaming when datasets exceed device VRAM ($\text{SF}=10, 30, \text{and } 100\text{ GB}$), out-of-core host RAM disk spilling \cite{kuiper2024duckdb_ooc}, and hardware energy efficiency ($\text{Joules}$ and $\text{MB/J}$).
    \item \textbf{Research Gap 3 (Confounded Complexity, Data Leakage, and Lack of Nested Cross-Workload Validation):} Traditional OLAP evaluations \cite{ahmed2020hibench, abdalla2022bigdata} rely on unstratified query sets where structural operator depth, storage layout (row-store vs. Parquet vs. Arrow), and predicate selectivity are confounded, and where random train-test splits across variations of the same query template induce severe data leakage without testing generalization to unseen seeds, join-crossing skew correlations \cite{boncz2017jcch}, or nested cross-validation on standard TPC-H Q1--Q22 choke-point workloads \cite{boncz2014tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}.
\end{itemize}

\par\vspace{7pt plus 2pt minus 1pt}\noindent\textbf{Bridging Memory-Wall Big Data Orchestration, Crossover Profiling, and Risk-Aware Routing.}
In modern hybrid analytics, "Big Data" scale is defined not solely by petabyte cluster volume, but by the ratio of query working-set footprint to local memory-hierarchy capacity ($\mathcal{B}_{\text{work}}(q)/M_{\text{cap}} \in [0.05\times, 6.25\times]$, spanning in-VRAM execution at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, out-of-VRAM PCIe chunked streaming at $\text{SF}=10\text{--}30\text{ GB}$, and heavy out-of-core NVMe spilling vs.\ elastic serverless cloud offloading at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ [$866.1\text{M}$ rows]). Designing a reliable cross-paradigm dispatcher across this continuum requires resolving a fundamental systems tension: static single-engine policies suffer severe performance cliffs across heterogeneous operators, yet naive point-estimate cost minimization (unconstrained $\arg\min$ over predicted latency $\hat{T}_e(q)$) triggers costly tail-latency spikes whenever prediction intervals overlap near CPU--GPU PCIe crossover boundaries or device memory ceilings. Rather than treating hardware benchmarking and learned routing in isolation, this study grounds an interpretable, zero-execution routing architecture directly in controlled factorial crossover telemetry within a well-defined hardware envelope ($4.0\text{ GB}$ PCIe Gen4 discrete GPU, $16\text{ GB}$ host DRAM with $9.0\text{ GB}$ DBMS cap, cross-checked on 22-thread \texttt{x86\_64} and Apple M1 \texttt{ARM64} nodes). Crucially, decoupling the pre-execution structural/volume representation ($\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^9$, extended to $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}}\in\mathbb{R}^{10}$) from the downstream decision policy exposes an explicit operational trade-off between \emph{unconstrained mean-latency minimization} and \emph{tail-safe SLA protection} (governed via calibrated conformal prediction intervals and resource-constrained Pareto dispatch). The primary contributions of this study are organized around three core pillars:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=4pt]
    \item \textbf{Claim 1 --- Cross-Paradigm Crossover Characterization via Pre-Execution AST and Zone-Map Profiling:} Across $213$ benchmark queries from seven suites ($97$ core TPC-H benchmark queries---$75$ factorial queries across $25$ canonical seeds $\times$ $3$ selectivity horizons in tiers L1--L5 plus $22$ official TPC-H queries Q1--Q22---and $116$ external cross-schema queries across five suites: $33$ IMDB JOB, $13$ SSB, $20$ ClickBench, $30$ TPC-DS-derived snowflake queries, and $20$ BIRD-inspired synthetic financial queries, or $228$ queries including $15$ prospective Seed~\texttt{S6} holdouts) and four scale factors ($\text{SF}\in\{1,10,30,100\}\text{ GB}$), we demonstrate that no single static engine dominates across operator families or memory regimes. While DuckDB leads on official TPC-H Q1--Q22 ($22.82\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $42.79\text{ ms}$ on Polars) due to correlated subquery unnesting, Rust Apache Arrow engines (Polars, DataFusion) and discrete CUDA GPUs dominate multi-way joins, window framing, and array unnesting whenever post-pushdown scanned volume amortizes PCIe Host-to-Device transfer overhead ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$).
    \item \textbf{Claim 2 --- Designated Primary Evaluation Endpoints (Tail-Risk Compression and Net Latency Over Static CPU Baselines):} To structure statistical inference transparently, we designate two primary evaluation endpoints at the independent seed-cluster level ($K=25$ seeds at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$): \emph{Primary Endpoint E1} (95th-percentile relative and absolute routing regret reduction vs.\ Always GPU) and \emph{Primary Endpoint E2} (paired net end-to-end latency reduction vs.\ the best single CPU engine under Holm--Bonferroni correction), while evaluating multi-scale $\text{SF}=10\text{--}100\text{ GB}$ scaling, energy/FinOps Pareto frontiers, and cross-schema transfer as secondary endpoints. Pairing our 9D multi-output cost regressor ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$, $4.15\text{ ms}$ cold-SQL full pipeline; $0.67\text{ ms}$ AST-only template cache; $0.08\text{ ms}$ 1D threshold cache) with a PCIe relative-margin guard (nested-CV modal $\hat{\tau}_{\text{guard}}^{(-k)}=8\%$: $55.37\text{ ms}$ net [$51.22\text{ ms}$ exec]; canonical fixed $\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$: $54.92\text{ ms}$ net [$50.78\text{ ms}$ exec]) and conformal/memory feasibility gates (Rules U2--U4) establishes formal two-sided $\pm 5.0\text{ ms}$ TOST equivalence under nested-CV ($55.37\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $56.96\text{ ms}$ Always GPU, $p_{\text{TOST}}=0.0442$, $p_{\text{non-inf}}=0.0012$) and one-sided non-inferiority under canonical $\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$ ($54.92\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{non-inf}}=0.00067$, $\pm 6.0\text{ ms}$ TOST $p_{\text{TOST},\pm 6\text{ms}}=0.0241$), compresses P95 relative regret by $2.17\times\text{--}2.25\times$ ($118.40\%$ nested-CV and $114.11\%$ canonical vs.\ $257.31\%$ on Always GPU; P95 absolute regret $35.90\text{ ms}$ / $34.51\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $51.42\text{ ms}$, full-workload $p_{\text{raw,seed}}=0.0098, p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0196$; dispatched subset $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0042$), and outperforms static CPU baselines by $1.50\times\text{--}3.35\times$ ($p_{\text{Holm,seed}}\le 0.0171$). Relative to the $27.55\text{ ms}$ realized six-engine routed latency reduction over Polars ($82.47 \rightarrow 54.92\text{ ms}$), $48.5\%$ ($13.35\text{ ms}$) is achieved purely by routing among standard SQL engines ($M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$: $69.12\text{ ms}$, $74.67\%$ Top-1, $p_{\text{Holm}}=0.0084$), while a minimal two-engine subset (Polars or DuckDB + GPU: $55.28\text{--}55.66\text{ ms}$) captures $97.3\%\text{--}98.7\%$ of the realized routed gain ($87.3\%\text{--}88.7\%$ of the $39.83\text{ ms}$ theoretical six-engine oracle reduction).
    \item \textbf{Claim 3 --- Operational Scope Boundaries Across Unseen Operators, Data Skew, Hardware Nodes, and Concurrency:} We delineate both generalization strengths and scope boundaries across three physical hardware nodes (8-thread \texttt{x86\_64}+GPU Node~1, 16-core/22-thread \texttt{x86\_64} CPU-only Node~2, and Apple M1 \texttt{ARM64} Node~3; detailed per-node protocols in Additional File~1, Table~S11) and $C=1\text{--}32$ concurrent streams ($2.14\times$ throughput gain to $154.44\text{ QPS}$). While locked zero-shot transfer succeeds on prospective seed holdouts (\texttt{S6}: $73.33\%$ Top-1), star schemas (SSB: $92.31\%$ Top-1), web analytics (ClickBench: $80.00\%$ Top-1), multi-channel snowflake rollups (TPC-DS-derived: $93.33\%$ Top-1), and financial ratio queries (BIRD-inspired: $90.00\%$ Top-1), encountering unseen operator classes under Leave-One-Tier-Out CV ($85.42\text{ ms}$) or unanchored string skew on IMDB JOB ($21.21\%$ Top-1) requires an Out-of-Distribution (OOD) structural fallback gate ($76.18\text{ ms}$ on LOTO-CV, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0312$ vs.\ Polars) or post-hoc exploratory $10.18\text{ KB}$ catalog sketches ($69.70\%$ Top-1 on JOB). Table~\ref{tab:key_findings_glance} synthesizes each claim alongside its empirical evidence and scope boundary.
\end{enumerate}

Primary Endpoint E1: Tail-Risk Control Over Static GPU Offload} & Equivalent within $\pm 5.0\text{ ms}$ under nested-CV $8\%$ ($55.37\text{ ms}$ net, TOST $p_{\text{TOST}}=0.0442$) and non-inferior under fixed $12\%$ ($54.92\text{ ms}$ net vs.\ $56.96\text{ ms}$ GPU, $p_{\text{non-inf}}=0.00067$) while cutting P95 relative regret by $\mathbf{2.17\times\text{--}2.25\times}$ ($\mathbf{257.31\% \rightarrow 118.40\% / 114.11\%}$) and P95 absolute regret from $\mathbf{51.42\text{ ms} \rightarrow 35.90 / 34.51\text{ ms}}$ (full $N=75$ $p_{\text{raw,seed}}=0.0098$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0196$; dispatched $n=24$ $p=0.0042$). & Unguarded point-estimate regressors (Ridge: $49.12\text{ ms}$ exec / $52.77\text{ ms}$ net; Shallow DT: $51.35\text{ ms}$ exec / $55.00\text{ ms}$ net) suffer $150.9\%\text{--}196.8\%$ P95 regret; when paired with the $+12\%$ guard ($55.49\text{ ms}$ / $56.55\text{ ms}$ net, $138.5\% / 128.4\%$ P95), $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ leads in both net latency and P95 regret. \\
\textbf{2. Primary Endpoint E2: Pure-SQL Pool Gain \& Minimal Engine Subsets} & Beats Always Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0171$), DataFusion ($96.30\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0041$), and DuckDB ($183.74\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}<0.001$). Pure native-SQL routing ($M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$, $0\%$ tensor ops) achieves $69.12\text{ ms}$ ($74.67\%$ Top-1, $p_{\text{Holm}}=0.0084$), capturing $\mathbf{48.5\%}$ of the $27.55\text{ ms}$ realized routed gain ($33.5\%$ of oracle). & Our GPU backend is an author-developed PyTorch CUDA tensor prototype on a $4\text{ GB}$ laptop RTX 2050 rather than a standalone SQL DBMS. A 2-engine subset (Polars or DuckDB + GPU) captures $\mathbf{97.3\%\text{--}98.7\%}$ of the realized routed gain ($\mathbf{87.3\%\text{--}88.7\%}$ of the $39.83\text{ ms}$ 6-engine oracle gain). \\
\textbf{3. Oblique Structural Index ($\text{CSCI}$) \& Tree Split Compression} & Within-tier partial correlation with GPU speedup is $\rho_{\text{partial}}=0.2653$ ($p=0.023$). Adding $\text{CSCI}(q)$ to 8D raw AST + selectivity (M3 vs.\ M2) prunes $-28.2\%$ of depth-3 GBDT splits, lowers regret by $-9.22\text{ pp}$ ($p=0.0038$, $52.1\%$ of $8\text{D}\rightarrow 9\text{D}$ feature gain), and halves calibration error ($\text{ECE}: 8.42\%\rightarrow 4.18\%$). & Inter-tier Kruskal--Wallis separation ($H=51.69, p<0.001$) is a structural design verification check because tiers L1--L5 were stratified by operator family; true predictive value is established by M3 vs.\ M2 ablation and external transfer. \\
\textbf{4. Cross-Schema Transfer Across 5 External Suites ($N_{\text{ext}}=116$)} & Strict zero-shot 9D transfer (trained only on $75$ TPC-H queries) achieves $92.31\%$ Top-1 on SSB ($N=13$), $80.00\%$ on ClickBench ($N=20$), $93.33\%$ on TPC-DS-derived snowflake ($N=30$), $90.00\%$ on BIRD-inspired financial ($N=20$), and safe Polars abstention on IMDB JOB ($N=33$, $6.75\text{ ms}$, $21.21\%$ Top-1). & Min/max zone maps are blind to unanchored \texttt{LIKE '\%...\%'} substrings and Zipfian join skew on IMDB JOB; resolving JOB to $69.70\%$ Top-1 ($4.54\text{ ms}$) requires a post-hoc exploratory $10.18\text{ KB}$ trigram/MCV catalog sketch ($\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{skew}}\in\mathbb{R}^{12}$). \\
\textbf{5. Multi-Scale Memory Walls ($\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$) \& Overhead Break-Even} & Achieves $1.04\times\text{--}1.07\times$ speedups over the best single engine per scale ($3.35\times\text{--}7.61\times$ over DuckDB) from $6\text{M}$ to $600.1\text{M}$ rows. At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, AST-Route ($1{,}142.80\text{ ms}$) saves $\mathbf{56.80\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ queries vs.\ Always GPU ($1{,}199.60\text{ ms}$) and $\mathbf{5{,}408.6\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ ($\mathbf{1.50\text{ hr}}$; $4{,}974.2\text{ s}/1\text{k}$ under scan/join-skewed mix) vs.\ Always Polars ($6{,}551.42\text{ ms}$). & Cold full router pipeline ($4.15\text{ ms}$: $3.48\text{ ms}$ \texttt{SQLGlot} parse $+ 0.17\text{ ms}$ zone-map $+ 0.50\text{ ms}$ GBR) requires query runtimes $>14.80\text{ ms}$ to break even; sub-$15\text{ ms}$ workloads use an AST-only template cache ($0.67\text{ ms}$, $T_{\text{BE}}=2.41\text{ ms}$) or 1D threshold cache ($0.08\text{ ms}$, $T_{\text{BE}}=0.29\text{ ms}$). \\
\textbf{6. Unseen Operator Extrapolation \& Cross-Node Calibration Scope} & Under Leave-One-Tier-Out CV (holding out an entire SQL operator family), our OOD structural domain gate reduces latency from $85.42\text{ ms}$ to $76.18\text{ ms}$ (beating Polars at $82.47\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0312$). & Tree regressors cannot extrapolate to unseen operator classes without the OOD gate, and migrating across CPU thread counts ($8\text{T}\rightarrow 22\text{T}$) requires lightweight $5$-seed log-ratio calibration ($2.74\text{ s}$ one-time) or native retraining. \\

\section{Related Work and Architectural Representativeness}
\subsection{Cloud MPP Warehouses, Distributed Query Engines, and Vectorized CPU Systems}
Disaggregated cloud data warehouses pioneered by Google Dremel/BigQuery \cite{melnik2010dremel, melnik2020dremel} and distributed MPP query engines such as Presto/Trino \cite{sethi2019presto}, ClickHouse \cite{schulze2024clickhouse}, and Apache Spark \cite{ahmed2020hibench} decouple compute workers from durable storage. Recent studies in serverless data pipelines \cite{shojaee2024serverless} and in-situ serverless query processing such as FunDa \cite{lounissi2025funda} highlight the fundamental tension between cold-start scheduling overhead and horizontal slot elasticity. Specifically, while Trino \cite{sethi2019presto} excels at federated cross-source query execution over distributed object stores and ClickHouse \cite{schulze2024clickhouse} achieves high throughput via shared-nothing merge-tree storage and SIMD vectorization across cluster nodes, both introduce network serialization and coordinator scheduling overheads when deployed on single-node or moderate-scale datasets. Conversely, embeddable C++ vectorized engines such as DuckDB \cite{raasveldt2019duckdb, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} process columnar Row Groups in-process using morsel-driven parallelism and out-of-core external memory management, whereas next-generation Rust engines (Polars and Apache DataFusion \cite{lamb2024datafusion}) execute zero-copy SIMD and streaming pipelines directly over standardized Apache Arrow memory buffers.

\subsection{GPU-Accelerated Databases, Learned Query Optimizers, and Adaptive Query Routing}
In the domain of GPU-accelerated relational analytics, systems such as HeavyDB (formerly OmniSci/MapD) \cite{root2019mapd}, BlazingSQL over RAPIDS cuDF \cite{kim2022blazingsql}, Crystal \cite{shanbhag2020crystal}, and HetExchange \cite{chrysogelos2019hetexchange} leverage massive SIMT parallelism and high-bandwidth device memory (VRAM) to accelerate relational joins and group-by aggregations. However, as demonstrated by Chrysogelos et al. \cite{chrysogelos2019hetexchange} and Shanbhag et al. \cite{shanbhag2020crystal}, discrete GPU execution is fundamentally constrained by the PCIe interconnect when data resides in host DRAM or exceeds device VRAM capacity, necessitating rigorous separation of PCIe transfer latency from kernel compute time.

Concurrently, recent surveys on deep learning in query execution \cite{milicevic2024dlquery} and learned query optimization have advanced along three distinct trajectories: (i) \emph{Intra-DBMS Plan Steering} systems such as Neo \cite{marcus2019neo} and Bao \cite{marcus2021bao}, which use tree-convolutional neural networks to generate end-to-end physical execution plans (Neo) or select contextual-bandit optimizer hint sets via Thompson sampling (Bao) inside a single relational engine (e.g., PostgreSQL) but cannot route queries across disparate execution paradigms; (ii) \emph{Learned Physical Cost Models} such as Zero-Shot Cost Models \cite{hilprecht2022zeroshot}, which generalize cost prediction across unseen single-node databases, yet still require compiled physical operator plan graphs and exact cardinalities rather than pre-execution raw SQL syntax; and (iii) \emph{Cross-Platform / Polystore Query Optimizers and Stage Routers} such as BigDAWG \cite{tan2023stage} and RHEEM / Apache Wayang \cite{gupta2023adaptive}, which decompose and dispatch compiled logical/physical sub-plans across heterogeneous data processing platforms (e.g., Spark, Java Streams, and PostgreSQL) using cost-based plan enumeration or historical execution logs, but require operator-graph compilation and do not combine zero-plan-compilation raw-SQL AST + zone-map dispatch with calibrated conformal tail-risk guards across local Rust Arrow, discrete PCIe GPU ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$), and serverless cloud warehouses. In parallel, the TPC-H decision support benchmark specification \cite{tpc2022tpch} establishes discrete scale factors ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}$) and 22 canonical queries (Q1--Q22) whose internal execution choke points (multi-way join graphs in Q2, Q5, Q7, Q8, Q9, Q21 and correlated subqueries in Q16--Q22) have been formalized by Boncz et al. \cite{boncz2014tpch} and Dreseler et al. \cite{dreseler2020quantifying}. Complementing classical OLAP benchmarks such as SSB \cite{oneil2009ssb} and JOB \cite{leis2015job}, modern text-to-SQL benchmarks such as BIRD \cite{li2023bird} and Spider 2.0 \cite{lei2025spider2} incorporate the Valid Efficiency Score ($\text{VES}(q,e) = \mathbb{I}(\text{EX}(q,e)=1)\cdot\sqrt{T_{\text{ref}}(q)/T_e(q)}\times 100$, exceeding $100$ whenever $T_e(q) < T_{\text{ref}}(q)$), while AST transpilers such as \texttt{SQLGlot} \cite{mao2024sqlglot} and Apache Calcite \cite{begoli2018calcite} enable deterministic structural analysis. Most recently, modular heterogeneous execution engines such as Maximus~\cite{kabic2025maximus} and DuckDB-integrated GPU accelerators such as Sirius~\cite{yogatama2025sirius} have demonstrated substantial SIMT speedups when relational working sets fit within device HBM/VRAM, while energy-aware and risk-aware learned query optimization frameworks such as E-LQO~\cite{liang2026elqo} have highlighted the importance of energy telemetry and prediction uncertainty within single-engine optimizers. Table~\ref{tab:related_work_comparison} summarizes how AST-Route advances beyond these intra-DBMS optimizers, GPU executors, zero-shot cost models, and cross-platform/polystore optimizers.

\subsection{Rationale for Target Execution Engines in the AST-Route Framework}
Rather than exhaustively evaluating every commercial DBMS, \textbf{AST-Route} targets six software engines that serve as canonical, non-redundant representatives of the four foundational execution paradigms in modern data engineering:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{SQLite 3 (Canonical Tuple-at-a-Time B-Tree Row-Store Reference Baseline):} Represents traditional ANSI C row-oriented storage using a Volcano-style iterator model, establishing the exact performance ($8417.33\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) and energy baseline of B-tree page traversal without columnar compression or SIMD vectorization. Because columnar CPU and GPU engines strictly dominate B-tree row-store scans on $100\%$ ($75/75$) of analytical queries, SQLite~3 receives a $0.0\%$ selection share in active routing while remaining in the six-engine benchmark matrix as the canonical row-store reference.
    \item \textbf{Google BigQuery (Canonical Serverless Disaggregated Cloud OLAP):} Represents petabyte-scale cloud-native execution (Dremel/Capacitor columnar storage with dynamic slot allocation) \cite{melnik2020dremel}, isolating distributed cloud orchestration overhead and storage clustering FinOps economics without local hardware bottlenecks.
    \item \textbf{DuckDB (Canonical C++ Morsel-Driven Columnar DBMS):} Represents state-of-the-art in-process C++ analytical database design \cite{raasveldt2019duckdb, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} with tightly integrated storage block management, parallel segment-tree window framing, and out-of-core external disk spilling.
    \item \textbf{Polars \& Apache DataFusion (Canonical Rust Apache Arrow Engines):} Represent the emerging Rust Arrow ecosystem via two distinct optimizer philosophies: \emph{Polars} exemplifies a dataframe-native lazy streaming engine over Arrow arrays, whereas \emph{Apache DataFusion} \cite{lamb2024datafusion} exemplifies a modular SQL physical query planner (used as the core execution engine in modern distributed systems such as InfluxDB 3.0 and Ballista).
    \item \textbf{Author-Developed PyTorch CUDA Relational Tensor Execution Backend (Compiled SIMT GPU Prototype):} Unlike DuckDB, DataFusion, SQLite, or BigQuery---which are standalone SQL database engines with integrated SQL parsers and query planners---this backend is an \emph{author-developed relational tensor execution prototype} built on PyTorch ATen/CUDA kernels over contiguous VRAM buffers (architecturally analogous to the operator kernels in BlazingSQL \cite{kim2022blazingsql} and Crystal \cite{shanbhag2020crystal}). Because our experimental testbed targets a tight $4.0\text{ GB}$ physical VRAM ceiling ($3.2\text{ GB}$ usable buffer on the laptop RTX~2050) that requires custom $4\times\text{--}40\times$ PyArrow-to-VRAM chunked streaming at $\text{SF}=10\text{--}100\text{ GB}$ and explicit hardware instrumentation of PCIe Gen4 H2D/D2H DMA transfer versus SIMT kernel execution (whereas monolithic GPU SQL engines such as HeavyDB \cite{root2019mapd}, RAPIDS cuDF \cite{kim2022blazingsql}, and Sirius \cite{yogatama2025sirius} target larger server-grade VRAM pools without exposing fine-grained 7-stage PCIe/kernel timers across out-of-VRAM chunks), this prototype compiles six core relational algebra operators (projection, boolean mask filtering, radix hash-join, segmented group-by, bitonic/radix sort, and prefix-scan window/unnest expansion) from \texttt{SQLGlot} AST plans. To ensure that AST-Route's scientific contribution never depends on backend implementation differences between compiled tensor kernels and SQL parsers, we enforce a three-level GPU timing protocol ($22.25\text{ ms}$ Level-1 Warm Kernel, $56.96\text{ ms}$ Level-2 Cold In-Memory PCIe+Kernel, and $61.11\text{ ms}$ Level-3 Full SQL-to-Result with $+4.15\text{ ms}$ lowering; $131.79\text{ ms}$ from cold on-disk Parquet) and evaluate \textbf{Pure Native-SQL Routing ($\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL-Only}}$, $0\%$ GPU prototype)} across all three hardware nodes and all seven benchmark suites as a co-equal primary finding (Section~\ref{sec:v852_reviewer_audit} and Additional File~1 (Table~S17)).
\end{enumerate}

% =============================================================================
% 3. THE PROPOSED AST-ROUTE FRAMEWORK ARCHITECTURE
% =============================================================================
\section{The Proposed AST-Route Framework Architecture}
As illustrated in Fig.~\ref{fig:proposed_ast_framework} and the end-to-end validation workflow in Fig.~\ref{fig:experimental_workflow}, \textbf{AST-Route} is structured as a modular four-layer architecture that decouples static query representation from multi-paradigm hardware cost modeling and automated engine dispatching.

\subsection{Layer 1: Static AST Structural Profiling, Five-Tier Workload, and Official TPC-H Scale Tiers ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}$)}
Given an incoming SQL query $q \in \mathcal{Q}$, Layer 1 parses the SQL statement into a canonical Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) using \texttt{SQLGlot} \cite{mao2024sqlglot} without touching underlying data pages. Traversing the AST yields seven structural operator dimensions: tree depth $D(q)$, total AST operator nodes $N(q)$, relational join count $J(q)$, nested CTE/subquery depth $S(q)$, window frame specifications $W(q)$, repeated field unnesting operators $U(q)$, and conditional \texttt{CASE} branches $C(q)$. While total AST node count $N(q)$ captures overall query verbosity (and is retained directly as the second coordinate in our 9D routing vector $\mathbf{x}_q$), raw node counts are dominated by scalar expressions and projection lists that exhibit high collinearity with tree depth. To isolate the non-linear algorithmic complexity of relational operators without expression-length inflation, we formulate the Composite Structural Complexity Index ($\text{CSCI}$) over the six core structural operators:
\begin{equation}
    \text{CSCI}(q) = 1.5 D(q) + 2.5 J(q) + 3.0 S(q) + 3.5 W(q) + 4.0 U(q) + 1.5 C(q)
\end{equation}
In accordance with the official TPC-H benchmark specification Clause 4.1.3 \cite{tpc2022tpch} and established multi-scale choke-point methodologies \cite{boncz2014tpch, dreseler2020quantifying, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc}, we provision the 8-table TPC-H relational schema across four standard discrete scale factors designed to systematically stress every layer of the modern memory hierarchy:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ (In-VRAM \& In-DRAM Baseline Regime):} Contains $6.00\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($8.66\times 10^6$ total rows across 8 tables). The working set fits entirely within both the $4.0\text{ GB}$ discrete GPU VRAM buffer ($1.30\text{ GB}$ peak) and the $16.0\text{ GB}$ host DRAM ($4.71\text{ GB}$ peak) with zero disk spilling.
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$ (Out-of-VRAM GPU Streaming \& In-DRAM Host Regime):} Contains $60.0\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($86.6\times 10^6$ total rows). Uncompressed working projections exceed the $4.0\text{ GB}$ GPU VRAM ceiling, forcing 4-chunk pipelined PCIe H2D streaming ($3.45\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM) while remaining primarily in host DRAM ($7.85\text{ GB}$ peak RAM, $125.0\text{ MB}$ minor spill).
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=30\text{ GB}$ (Host DRAM Cap Inflection \& Moderate Out-of-Core Spill Regime):} Contains $180.0\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($259.8\times 10^6$ total rows) \cite{tpc2022tpch}. Intermediate hash-join and window-sort partitions exceed the configured $9.00\text{ GB}$ host DBMS buffer pool cap, triggering active out-of-core disk spilling \cite{kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} ($864.0\text{ MB}$ mean spill) and 12-chunk GPU PCIe streaming ($3.68\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM).
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ (Extreme Out-of-Core Spilling \& Cloud Elasticity Crossover Regime):} Contains $600.1\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($866.1\times 10^6$ total rows) \cite{tpc2022tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}, inducing heavy external disk spilling on local CPU engines ($3302.0\text{ MB}$ mean spill, peaking at $6840.0\text{ MB}$ in L2 joins) and 40-chunk PCIe transfers on the GPU ($3.85\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM).
\end{itemize}

Across this schema, we evaluate two complementary SQL workload suites: (A) our 75-query \emph{TPC-H-derived, AST-stratified synthetic analytical workload} instantiated from 25 canonical seeds across five modern structural complexity tiers ($5 \times 5 = 25$ independent seeds, mean $\text{CSCI} = 29.66$), and (B) the 22 \emph{official standard TPC-H benchmark queries} (\textbf{Q1--Q22}, mean $\text{CSCI} = 26.52$, range $12.0\text{--}47.5$) \cite{tpc2022tpch, boncz2014tpch}. Because the base TPC-H schema consists of flat relational tables, Tier L5 dynamically constructs nested array columns in-query via staged \texttt{ARRAY\_\allowbreak{}AGG} / \texttt{LIST} aggregation over one-to-many \texttt{orders}--\texttt{lineitem} relationships before benchmarking lateral \texttt{CROSS JOIN UNNEST} re-aggregation:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{L1 (Scan, SARGability, \& Monotonic Conditional Branching):} Single-table columnar scans with multi-branch \texttt{CASE WHEN} expressions and distinct cardinality aggregations ($D = 9.0, J = 0.0, \text{CSCI} = 15.3$).
    \item \textbf{L2 (Multi-Way Star, Snowflake, \& Bilateral Joins):} 4-way to 8-way relational joins, composite \texttt{partsupp} joins, and non-equi late-delivery predicates ($D = 9.2, J = 4.8, \text{CSCI} = 26.8$).
    \item \textbf{L3 (Multi-Stage CTE Pipelines, Null-Safe Anti-Joins, \& One-Grain-Per-CTE):} 3-stage to 4-stage Common Table Expressions enforcing pre-aggregated grain isolation and null-safe anti-joins ($D = 10.8, J = 2.6, \text{CSCI} = 36.7$).
    \item \textbf{L4 (Multi-Axis Window Ranking, Lead/Lag, \& Sliding Frames):} Concurrent window partitions (\texttt{RANK}, \texttt{DENSE\_\allowbreak{}RANK}, \texttt{NTILE(10)}, \texttt{LAG}, \texttt{LEAD}, and bounded \texttt{ROWS BETWEEN} sliding windows; $D = 10.8, J = 1.6, \text{CSCI} = 37.1$).
    \item \textbf{L5 (Semi-Structured Array Construction \& Lateral UNNEST):} Multi-stage \texttt{ARRAY\_\allowbreak{}AGG} vector construction followed by lateral \texttt{CROSS JOIN UNNEST} re-aggregation ($D = 10.6, J = 2.2, \text{CSCI} = 32.4$).
\end{enumerate}

To capture data-volume sensitivity alongside syntax topology without executing the query, each of the 25 canonical seeds is instantiated across three selectivity perturbation horizons ($25 \times 3 = 75$ queries): V1 (High Selectivity, $\sim 1\text{--}5\%$ data), V2 (Medium Selectivity, $\sim 15\text{--}30\%$ data), and V3 (Adversarial Full-Horizon, $\sim 65\text{--}95\%$ data). As detailed in Table~\ref{tab:metadata_scope_audit}, Layer 1 extracts the pre-execution predicate selectivity estimate $\hat{\sigma}(q) \in (0, 1]$ in $172.0\text{ }\mu\text{s}$ from a pre-cached metadata snapshot ($412.6\text{ }\mu\text{s}$ cold footer read on Apache Parquet; $845.2\text{ }\mu\text{s}$ live \texttt{PRAGMA storage\_\allowbreak{}info} query on DuckDB) strictly prior to query execution without scanning data blocks or touching post-execution results. Crucially, we distinguish the exact metadata capabilities and limitations of each storage engine: (i) \emph{Parquet and DuckDB Row-Group Zone Maps}: \texttt{pyarrow.parquet.ParquetFile.metadata} and DuckDB \texttt{PRAGMA storage\_\allowbreak{}info('table')} expose column-level minimum, maximum, and null-count statistics across $100\%$ of numeric and date columns (e.g., \texttt{l\_\allowbreak{}shipdate} $\in [\texttt{1992-01-02}, \texttt{1998-12-01}]$, \texttt{l\_\allowbreak{}quantity} $\in [1.0, 50.0]$), enabling accurate range interpolation on both partitioned and non-partitioned numeric predicates (comparing estimated $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ against true post-execution row selectivity $\sigma_{\text{actual}}(q) = |R_{\text{out}}|/|R_{\text{table}}|$ yields a mean absolute error of $\text{MAE}=3.84\%$ and median Q-Error $=1.092$ (P90 $= 1.284$) across the 75-query suite, and $\text{MAE}=1.52\%$, Q-Error $=1.263$ on non-partitioned multi-attribute numeric predicates); (ii) \emph{BigQuery Partition Pruning Statistics}: \texttt{INFORMATION\_\allowbreak{}SCHEMA.}\newline\texttt{PARTITIONS} and dry-run byte estimation provide \emph{partition pruning and clustering statistics} (\texttt{partition\_\allowbreak{}id}, \texttt{total\_\allowbreak{}rows}, \texttt{total\_\allowbreak{}logical\_\allowbreak{}bytes}) rather than general column-level min/max zone maps for every attribute. When predicates filter on the partition key (\texttt{l\_\allowbreak{}shipdate}), partition pruning metadata estimates selectivity within $\text{MAE}=4.26\%$; however, on non-partitioned columns where BigQuery does not expose column-level min/max bounds, AST-Route either references the shared Parquet footer catalog or falls back to parametric AST operator priors (which we explicitly evaluate in Table~\ref{tab:ablation_robustness} Exp~C by masking $50\%$ of metadata, sustaining $53.33\%$ Top-1 accuracy and $68.42\text{ ms}$ mean latency); and (iii) \emph{String Predicates and Skewed Distributions}: because continuous min/max zone maps cannot bound unanchored substring filters (\texttt{LIKE '\%brass\%'}) or capture intra-range Zipfian skew ($s=1.2$), estimation error increases to $\text{MAE}=5.00\%$ (Q-Error $=2.000$) on substring predicates and $\text{MAE}=38.8\%$ (Q-Error $=4.880$) under severe power-law skew (explaining the $21.21\%$ Top-1 accuracy on string-skewed IMDB JOB queries in Table~\ref{tab:priority3_validation} Part~B). Combining the seven static AST operator counts with $\text{CSCI}(q)$ and estimated selectivity $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ yields the 9-dimensional routing feature vector:
\begin{equation}
    \mathbf{x}_q = \big[\, D(q),\; N(q),\; J(q),\; S(q),\; W(q),\; U(q),\; C(q),\; \text{CSCI}(q),\; \hat{\sigma}(q) \,\big]^{\top} \in \mathbb{R}^{9}
\end{equation}
 To avoid any ambiguity between representations, $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$ serves as the sole feature vector for all fixed-scale ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) evaluations (Tables~\ref{tab:routing_evaluation}--\ref{tab:priority3_validation} and Additional File~1 (Table~S10)), whereas the scale-augmented vector $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}} = [\mathbf{x}_q^{\top}, \log_{10}(1+\hat{\sigma}(q)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}})]^{\top} \in \mathbb{R}^{10}$ is used exclusively in cross-scale training and dispatch across $\text{SF}\in\{1,10,30,100\}\text{ GB}$ (Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part~A and Additional File~1, Table~S7 Part~C), rather than mixing two competing models on the same experiment. Crucially, while $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^9$ encodes scale-invariant syntax topology and relative selectivity $\hat{\sigma}(q) \in (0,1]$, multi-scale awareness across $\text{SF} \in \{1, 10, 30, 100\}$ GB is achieved in Layers 2--4 by multiplying $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ with the pre-execution catalog table byte footprint $\mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}}$ ($\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\text{scan}}(q) = \hat{\sigma}(q)\cdot \mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}}$) inside the analytical memory/PCIe chunk estimator and the capacity filter $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)$ alongside scale-calibrated cost predictors.

Google BigQuery (Cloud) & \texttt{INFORMATION\_\allowbreak{}SCHEMA.}\newline\texttt{PARTITIONS} + dry-run & Partition ID, total rows, logical/billable bytes & $172.0\text{ }\mu\text{s}$ cached snapshot ($<0.20\text{ ms}$) & $\text{MAE}=4.26\%$ (Partition key \texttt{l\_\allowbreak{}shipdate}) & Partition/clustering pruning stats only; non-partitioned cols use Parquet catalog or training-fold prior \\
\hline
\multicolumn{6}{p{16.2cm}}{\footnotesize \textit{Incomplete-Metadata \& AST Overhead Audit (25-Seed LOSO-CV):} \texttt{SQLGlot} single-pass AST extraction requires $3.476\text{ ms}$ across the 75 benchmark SQL files ($0.172\text{ ms}$ cached zone-map lookup or $0.413\text{ ms}$ cold NVMe footer \texttt{seek(-8)}, plus $0.497\text{ ms}$ GBR inference; $4.145\text{ ms}$ total warm overhead vs.\ $70.90\text{ ms}$ uncached full-dataset schema open). Under 0\% missing metadata: $57.33\%$ Top-1 ($54.92\text{ ms}$); under 50\% masked zone maps (training-fold prior fallback): $53.33\%$ Top-1 ($68.42\text{ ms}$); under 100\% missing metadata (8D pure AST syntax): $52.00\%$ Top-1 ($73.22\text{ ms}$).} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{fitasttable}
\end{table*}
\endgroup

\subsection{Layer 2: Cross-Paradigm Analytical Cost, PCIe Crossover, and Device Energy Telemetry Formulation}
To enable apples-to-apples cost evaluation across serverless cloud, local CPU, and discrete GPU backends, Layer 2 establishes six analytical cost and hardware telemetry models:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=3pt]
    \item \textbf{CUDA Cold-Start Decomposition and PCIe Bottleneck Fraction ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$):} On discrete GPUs (NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2050, $4.0\text{ GB}$ VRAM), cold-start execution time $T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q)$ is decomposed into PCIe Host-to-Device transfer time ($T_{\text{PCIe\_H2D}}$) and pure in-VRAM kernel compute time ($T_{\text{GPU\_Warm}}$):
    \begin{equation}
        T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q) = \underbrace{T_{\text{PCIe\_H2D}}(q) + T_{\text{PCIe\_D2H}}(q)}_{T_{\text{PCIe}}(q)} + T_{\text{GPU\_Warm}}(q), \qquad \Phi_{\text{PCIe}}(q) = \frac{T_{\text{PCIe}}(q)}{T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q)} \times 100\%
    \end{equation}
    Cold-start GPU offloading outperforms vectorized CPU execution ($T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q) < T_{\text{CPU}}(q)$) if and only if the warm kernel speedup satisfies the break-even condition $\text{Speedup}_{\text{Warm}}(q) > (1 - \Phi_{\text{PCIe}}(q)/100)^{-1}$.
    \item \textbf{Effective Analytical Scan Throughput ($\Theta_{\text{eff}}$):} Normalizing unpruned columnar volume $\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)$ (in MB) against execution time $T_{e}(q)$ (in ms) yields effective processing throughput in $\text{GB/s}$:
    \begin{equation}
        \Theta_{\text{eff}}(q, e) = \frac{\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)}{1.024 \cdot T_{e}(q)} \quad [\text{GB/s}]
    \end{equation}
    \item \textbf{Cloud Slot-to-Wall-Time Concurrency Proxy ($\mathcal{M}_{\text{slot}}$) and FinOps Cost Savings ($\Delta C_{\text{1M}}$):} Quantifies cumulative distributed slot compute time relative to server wall-clock time on BigQuery (serving as an effective concurrency proxy), alongside financial savings per $10^6$ queries under on-demand bytes-scanned pricing ($\alpha_{\text{TB}} = \$6.25\text{ USD/TiB}$):
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{M}_{\text{slot}}(q) = \frac{T_{\text{BQ\_Slot}}(q)}{T_{\text{BQ\_Wall}}(q)}, \qquad \Delta C_{\text{1M}}(q) = 10^6 \times \left( \frac{\max(\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q), 10) - \max(\mathcal{B}_{\text{clustered}}(q), 10)}{1024^2} \right) \times \alpha_{\text{TB}}
    \end{equation}
    \item \textbf{Active Device Energy Telemetry ($E(q,e)$), 50-Repetition Bland--Altman Calibration, Three-Scope Boundary Separation, and Energy-Aware Routing Scope ($\eta_{\text{watt}}$):} High-frequency telemetry polling (200--250 Hz via \texttt{psutil}, NVIDIA NVML \texttt{nvmlDeviceGetPowerUsage}, and \texttt{torch.cuda} counters across $R=5$ timed benchmark repetitions per query (following $W=2$ warm-up runs, $75\times 5 = 375$ timed trials) and $R_{\text{cal}}=50$ DVFS calibration repetitions per workload tier, totaling $250$ calibration runs; measurement $\text{CV} = 2.84\%\text{--}3.52\%$) records active CPU utilization ($\text{CPU}_{\text{util}}\%$), peak host RAM ($\text{RAM}_{\text{used}}$ in GB), disk spilling ($\text{Spill}_{\text{disk}}$ in MB), GPU SM utilization ($\text{GPU}_{\text{util}}\%$), and peak device memory ($\text{VRAM}_{\text{used}}$ in GB). To maintain strict physical transparency across hardware interfaces, we separate energy metrics into three explicit boundary scopes and refrain from claiming whole-system wall-socket energy savings: (i) \emph{Measured GPU Board Energy ($E_{\text{GPU}}$)}: sampled directly from physical NVIDIA NVML on-board hardware power sensors ($10.5\text{ W}$ idle baseline; 50-repetition $\text{MAE} = 0.008\text{ J}$, $\text{RMSE} = 0.010\text{ J}$, relative error $\text{MACE} = 1.24\%$, Bland--Altman bias $+0.001\text{ J}$ with 95\% limits of agreement [LoA]: $[-0.018, +0.020]\text{ J}$); (ii) \emph{Estimated CPU Package Energy ($E_{\text{CPU}}$)}: because unprivileged ring-3 processes on Windows 11 cannot read ring-0 Intel RAPL / AMD SMU MSR energy registers directly without a kernel driver, active CPU package power is modeled via TDP-calibrated utilization polling $P_{\text{CPU}}(t) = P_{\text{idle}} + (P_{\text{TDP}} - P_{\text{idle}})\cdot(\text{CPU}_{\text{util}}(t)/100)$ ($14.5\text{ W}$ idle, $45.0\text{ W}$ TDP). Cross-validating this linear TDP-calibrated estimate against a non-linear CMOS DVFS voltage-frequency reference curve ($P \propto u^{1.28}$) across $R=50$ repetitions per workload tier yields an absolute error of $\text{MAE} = 0.205\text{ J}$ ($\text{RMSE} = 0.214\text{ J}$) and Bland--Altman bias of $-0.004\text{ J}$ (95\% LoA: $[-0.422, +0.414]\text{ J}$) on DuckDB ($6.40\text{ J}$ mean), and $\text{MAE} = 0.098\text{ J}$ ($\text{RMSE} = 0.102\text{ J}$, Bland--Altman bias $-0.002\text{ J}$, 95\% LoA: $[-0.201, +0.197]\text{ J}$) on Polars ($3.17\text{ J}$ mean), corresponding to a mean relative calibration deviation of $\text{MACE} = 3.09\%\text{--}3.21\%$ and rank concordance $\rho \ge 0.9982$ (we emphasize that this $3.09\%\text{--}3.21\%$ MACE bounds analytical curve-form sensitivity between linear TDP interpolation and non-linear DVFS scaling rather than certifying accuracy within $3.21\%$ of physical ring-0 RAPL/SMU hardware counters, which we complement macroscopically via 45-second ACPI DC battery-shunt discharge telemetry); and (iii) \emph{Composite Host+Device System Energy ($E_{\text{sys}} = E_{\text{GPU}} + E_{\text{CPU\_assist}}$)}: combines measured GPU board energy with estimated concurrent host CPU DMA/driver energy ($16.17\text{--}16.33\text{ W}$) during PCIe transfers ($1.58\text{ J}$ cold, $0.61\text{ J}$ warm; $\text{MAE} = 0.031\text{ J}$, $\text{MACE} = 1.96\%$, 95\% LoA: $[-0.064, +0.062]\text{ J}$). Because $E_{\text{CPU}}$ is an utilization-based package estimate rather than a ring-0 RAPL counter reading and excludes AC-DC PSU conversion, DRAM DIMM, and NVMe SSD rails (which require an external inline AC power meter), we explicitly designate CPU energy figures as \emph{Estimated CPU Package Energy} and frame our multi-objective dispatcher as \emph{energy-aware routing} rather than claiming physical whole-system energy certification. Integrating $\bar{P}_e(q)$ over execution duration $T_e(q)$ yields device energy in Joules and energy efficiency in $\text{MB/s/W} \equiv \text{MB/J}$:
\begin{equation}
        E(q, e) = \int_{t_0}^{t_0 + T_e(q)/1000} P_e(t)\,dt \approx \bar{P}_e(q) \cdot \left(\frac{T_e(q)}{1000}\right) \;\; [\text{Joules}], \qquad \eta_{\text{watt}}(q, e) = \frac{\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)}{E(q, e)} \;\; [\text{MB/J}]
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Layers 3 \& 4: Multi-Output Cost-Regression Predictor, Q-Error Metric, and Constrained Multi-Objective Dispatcher}
In Layer 3 (Fig.~\ref{fig:proposed_ast_framework}), \textbf{AST-Route} employs a Multi-Output Gradient Boosting Regressor $f_{\Theta}: \mathbb{R}^{9} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{M}$ to simultaneously predict log-transformed execution latency $\widehat{\ln(1 + T_e)}(\mathbf{x}_q)$, energy footprint $\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$, and memory consumption ($\widehat{\text{RAM}}_e, \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e$) across the six benchmarked backends $\mathcal{E}_{\text{all}} = \mathcal{E} \cup \{\text{SQLite 3}\}$, where SQLite~3 serves as the B-tree row-store reference baseline ($0\%$ selection share under cost minimization because it is strictly dominated on $75/75$ queries) and $\mathcal{E} = \{\text{DuckDB}, \text{Polars}, \text{DataFusion}, \text{GPU}_{\text{Cold}}, \text{BigQuery}\}$ forms the five active analytical routing candidates. Predictive accuracy of the cost model is evaluated via out-of-fold log-$R^2$, Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE), and the multiplicative \textbf{Q-Error} metric standard in database query optimizer research \cite{leis2015job, hilprecht2022zeroshot}:
\begin{equation}
    \text{Q-Error}(y, \hat{y}) = \max\!\left( \frac{\hat{y}}{y},\; \frac{y}{\hat{y}} \right) \ge 1.0
\end{equation}
In Layer 4, the dispatcher selects the optimal engine minimizing a memory-constrained, multi-objective Pareto cost function parameterized by user-configurable weights $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in [0, 1]$ ($\alpha + \beta + \gamma = 1$, where $\alpha$ weights normalized latency $\hat{T}_e/T_{\text{med}}$, $\beta$ weights normalized energy $\hat{E}_e/E_{\text{med}}$, and $\gamma$ weights normalized marginal cloud scan charge $\hat{C}_e/C_{\text{ref}}$) subject to hardware memory capacity budgets ($\widehat{\text{RAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{host\_max}}$ and $\widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{gpu\_max}}$). Let $e^*(q) = \operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{e \in \mathcal{E}} T_e(q)$ denote the ground-truth Oracle optimal engine:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    \hat{e}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(q) &= \operatorname*{arg\,min}_{e \in \mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \left[ \alpha \cdot \frac{\hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)}{T_{\text{med}}(q)} + \beta \cdot \frac{\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)}{E_{\text{med}}(q)} + \gamma \cdot \frac{\hat{C}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)}{C_{\text{ref}}(q)} \right], \\
    \mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}}(q) &= \frac{T_{\hat{e}(q)}(q) - T_{e^*(q)}(q)}{T_{e^*(q)}(q)} \times 100\%, \qquad \Delta T_{\text{abs}}(q) = T_{\hat{e}(q)}(q) - T_{e^*(q)}(q)
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where we report both relative percentage regret $\mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}}(q)$ and absolute millisecond regret $\Delta T_{\text{abs}}(q)$ so sub-$30\text{ ms}$ scan queries are never distorted by small denominators. To decouple query syntax from deployment hardware, each physical node is characterized by a five-dimensional \textbf{Hardware Descriptor Vector} $\mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}} = [M_{\text{host\_max}},\, M_{\text{gpu\_max}},\, B_{\text{PCIe}},\, N_{\text{CPU}},\, B_{\text{MEM}}]^{\top} \in \mathbb{R}^{5}$ encoding host DBMS RAM cap ($\text{GB}$), device VRAM cap ($\text{GB}$), sustained pinned PCIe DMA bandwidth ($\text{GB/s}$), logical CPU thread count, and host DRAM bandwidth ($\text{GB/s}$): specifically, Node~1 ($\mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}}^{(1)} = [9.0, 4.0, 5.69, 8, 25.6]^{\top}$), Node~2 ($\mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}}^{(2)} = [9.0, 0.0, 0.0, 22, 42.7]^{\top}$), and Node~3 Apple M1 \texttt{ARM64} ($\mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}}^{(3)} = [8.0, 0.0, 0.0, 8, 68.3]^{\top}$). Here $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q, \mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}}) = \{e \in \mathcal{E} \mid \widehat{\text{RAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{host\_max}} \land \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{gpu\_max}}\}$ prunes any candidate engine whose predicted memory footprint violates hardware capacity ceilings prior to objective scoring (automatically disabling GPU dispatch when $M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$), while few-shot cross-node transfer ($m \in \{1,3,5,10,15\}$ calibration queries in Section~\ref{sec:v852_reviewer_audit}) adjusts log-latency predictions via a per-engine hardware scaling offset $\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q, \mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}}) = \hat{z}_e^{\text{src}}(\mathbf{x}_q) + \hat{\delta}_e(\mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}})$, and each objective term is normalized by its cross-engine reference scale ($T_{\text{med}}(q)$, $E_{\text{med}}(q)$, and $C_{\text{ref}}(q) = \max(C_{\text{BQ}}(q), \epsilon)$). Per $10^6$ queries, we separate incremental \emph{On-Demand Cloud Query Scan Charge} $\hat{C}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$ ($\$0.00$ local vs.\ $(\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\text{clustered}}(\mathbf{x}_q)/2^{40})\times\$6.25/\text{TiB}$ on BigQuery), active device energy $\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$ ($\$0.02\text{--}\$0.21/1\text{M}$ at $\$0.12/\text{kWh}$), and sunk local workstation amortization ($\$0.58\text{--}\$1.94/1\text{M}$ at $\$0.038/\text{hr}$).

Specifically, predicted log-latencies $\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) = \widehat{\ln(1+T_e)}(\mathbf{x}_q)$ are mapped back to the millisecond domain via $\hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) = \max(\exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)) - 1, \epsilon)$. For each outer cross-validation fold $k$, AST-Route computes the empirical $(1-\alpha_{\text{conf}})=90\%$ split/cross-conformal quantile $\hat{q}_{0.90,e}^{(-k)} = \text{Quantile}_{0.90}\big(\{|\ln(1+T_e(q_i)) - \hat{z}_e^{(-k)}(\mathbf{x}_{q_i})|\}_{q_i \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{train}}^{(-k)}}\big)$ with finite-sample correction $\lceil(n_{\text{cal}}+1)(1-\alpha_{\text{conf}})\rceil/n_{\text{cal}}$ (alongside a complexity-stratified Mondrian conformal variant scaled by tier dispersion $\hat{\sigma}_{\text{tier}}(\text{CSCI}(q))$ to preserve conditional coverage across heterogeneous operator tiers, outperforming uncalibrated pinball-loss quantile regression \texttt{GBR-Quantile} in Table~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} Part~D) strictly inside the training fold, yielding the calibrated prediction interval:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conformal_interval}
[\hat{L}_{0.90,e}(q),\, \hat{U}_{0.90,e}(q)] = \Big[\max\!\big(\exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) - \hat{q}_{0.90,e}^{(-k)}) - 1,\, \epsilon\big),\; \exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) + \hat{q}_{0.90,e}^{(-k)}) - 1\Big]
\end{equation}
Crucially, because distribution-free conformal coverage $\mathbb{P}(T_e(q) \in [\hat{L}_{0.90,e}, \hat{U}_{0.90,e}]) \ge 1 - \alpha_{\text{conf}}$ theoretically holds under \emph{exchangeability} between calibration and test queries, encountering severe covariate or operator shift violates exchangeability unless paired with Mondrian stratification, 12D catalog sketches, and structural OOD fallback. To maintain strict terminology precision, we evaluate our uncertainty-aware abstention mechanism as a stepwise progression from \textbf{U0 (Unguarded $\arg\min$)} across four orthogonal rules summarized in Algorithm~1: (U1) \emph{Nested-CV Calibrated PCIe Relative-Margin Guard}, a relative cost-ratio guard that abstains from cold GPU dispatch to the fastest feasible vectorized CPU engine when $(\hat{T}_{\text{CPU}} - \hat{T}_{\text{GPU\_Cold}})/\hat{T}_{\text{GPU\_Cold}} < \tau_{\text{guard}}$ ($\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$ canonical; modal $8\%$ nested-CV; stepwise ablation U0 $\rightarrow$ U1/U2 $\rightarrow$ U3 $\rightarrow$ U1--U4 and sensitivity sweep $\tau_{\text{guard}}\in[0\%,30\%]$ evaluated in Table~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} Part~C); (U2) \emph{Conformal Cloud Upper-Bound Gate}, which uses the formal $90\%$ conformal upper bound $\hat{U}_{0.90,\text{BQ}}(q)$ from Eq.~\eqref{eq:conformal_interval} to abstain from serverless BigQuery offload unless $\hat{U}_{0.90,\text{BQ}}(q) < \min_{e \in \mathcal{E}_{\text{local}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \hat{T}_e(q)$ or local memory caps are exhausted; (U3) \emph{Unanchored String/Missing-Zone-Map Guard}, defaulting to in-memory Arrow CPU execution when unanchored \texttt{LIKE '\%...\%'} filters lack 12D catalog sketches; and (U4) \emph{Hard Memory-Wall Pruning} via $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q, \mathbf{h}_{\text{hw}})$ prior to objective scoring.

\begin{table}[!t]
\centering
\caption*{{\bf Algorithm 1:} Pre-Execution Risk-Aware Multi-Objective Query Dispatch (\textbf{AST-Route})}\label{alg:ast_route}
\footnotesize
\begin{fitasttable}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.97\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Input:} Raw SQL query $q$; catalog zone-map snapshot $\mathcal{C}_{\text{meta}}$ (optional 10.18~KB string/skew sketch $\mathcal{C}_{\text{sketch}}$); calibrated multi-output regressors $\{f_{\Theta,e}\}$ and fold conformal quantiles $\{\hat{q}_{0.90,e}\}$; Pareto weights $(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)$; memory caps $(M_{\text{host\_max}}, M_{\text{gpu\_max}})$; guard $\tau_{\text{guard}}=0.12$. \\
\textbf{Output:} Selected execution engine $\hat{e}(q) \in \mathcal{E}$ across the 5 active analytical candidates ($\mathcal{E} = \{\texttt{DuckDB}, \texttt{Polars}, \texttt{DataFusion}, \texttt{GPU\_Cold}, \texttt{BigQuery}\}$, with \texttt{SQLite~3} retained in $\mathcal{E}_{\text{all}}$ as the B-tree row-store reference baseline receiving $0\%$ dispatch share) with zero physical plan compilation prior to dispatch. \\
\midrule
1: \textbf{Static AST Profiling (Layer 1A):} Parse $q$ via \texttt{SQLGlot} in single pass to extract $[D(q),N(q),J(q),S(q),W(q),U(q),C(q)]$ and compute $\text{CSCI}(q)$ via Eq.~(1). \\
2: \textbf{Pre-Execution Selectivity Lookup (Layer 1B):} Query $\mathcal{C}_{\text{meta}}$ for range/partition predicates to obtain $\hat{\sigma}(q) \in (0,1]$ (if column bounds are absent or unanchored \texttt{LIKE}, apply training-fold operator prior or $\mathcal{C}_{\text{sketch}}$) and form $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$ (or $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}} \in \mathbb{R}^{10}$ across scales). \\
3: \textbf{Multi-Output Cost \& Interval Prediction (Layer 3):} For each $e \in \mathcal{E}$, predict $\hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) = \max(\exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q))-1,\epsilon)$, $\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$, $(\widehat{\text{RAM}}_e, \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e)$, marginal scan fee $\hat{C}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$, and $90\%$ conformal bounds $[\hat{L}_{0.90,e}(q), \hat{U}_{0.90,e}(q)]$ via Eq.~\eqref{eq:conformal_interval}. \\
4: \textbf{Feasibility Filtering (Rule U4):} $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q) \leftarrow \{e \in \mathcal{E} \mid \widehat{\text{RAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{host\_max}} \wedge \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{gpu\_max}}\}$; if $\mathcal{E}_{\text{local}} \cap \mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q) = \emptyset$, return $\texttt{BigQuery}$ (elastic cloud spill fallback). \\
5: \textbf{Pareto Candidate Scoring (Layer 4):} Compute $\hat{e}_0 \leftarrow \operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{e \in \mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \big[\alpha \hat{T}_e/T_{\text{med}} + \beta \hat{E}_e/E_{\text{med}} + \gamma \hat{C}_e/C_{\text{ref}}\big]$ via Eq.~(8). \\
6: \textbf{Conformal Abstention Guards (Rules U1--U3):} Let $e_{\text{cpu}}^\star \leftarrow \operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{e \in \{\texttt{DuckDB},\texttt{Polars},\texttt{DataFusion}\}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$. \\
\hspace*{1.2em}\textbf{if} $\hat{e}_0 = \texttt{GPU\_\allowbreak{}Cold}$ \textbf{and} $\big((\hat{T}_{e_{\text{cpu}}^\star} - \hat{T}_{\texttt{GPU\_\allowbreak{}Cold}})/\hat{T}_{\texttt{GPU\_\allowbreak{}Cold}} < \tau_{\text{guard}} \;\vee\; \text{UnanchoredStringNoSketch}(q)\big)$ \textbf{then} $\hat{e}_0 \leftarrow e_{\text{cpu}}^\star$. \\
\hspace*{1.2em}\textbf{if} $\hat{e}_0 = \texttt{BigQuery}$ \textbf{and} $\hat{U}_{0.90,\texttt{BQ}}(q) \ge \hat{T}_{e_{\text{cpu}}^\star}(\mathbf{x}_q)$ \textbf{then} $\hat{e}_0 \leftarrow e_{\text{cpu}}^\star$. \\
7: \textbf{return} $\hat{e}(q) \leftarrow \hat{e}_0$. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{fitasttable}
\end{table}

To rigorously prevent data leakage across query variations sharing the same template structure, we evaluate \textbf{AST-Route} under three complementary validation protocols: (i) \textbf{25-fold Leave-One-Seed-Out cross-validation (LOSO-CV)} across all 25 disjoint seed groups ($N=75$ queries), (ii) a \textbf{Strict Unseen Seed Holdout Split}, where the framework is trained exclusively on 20 development seeds (\texttt{S1}--\texttt{S4} across L1--L5, $N_{\text{dev}}=60$ queries) and evaluated once on 5 completely locked unseen test seeds (\texttt{L1\_\allowbreak{}S5, L2\_\allowbreak{}S5, L3\_\allowbreak{}S5, L4\_\allowbreak{}S5, L5\_\allowbreak{}S5}, $N_{\text{unseen}}=15$ queries), and (iii) \textbf{Strict Nested 22-fold Leave-One-Query-Out cross-validation (Nested LOO-CV)} on the 22 official standard TPC-H benchmark queries (\textbf{Q1--Q22}) \cite{tpc2022tpch}, where feature calibration weights are tuned strictly inside the 21-query inner training loop without touching the held-out test query. We benchmark \textbf{AST-Route} ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{RF-9D}}$ and $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$) against four baseline policies: \textbf{(1) Always DuckDB}, \textbf{(2) Always GPU (Cold-Start)}, \textbf{(3) Always BigQuery (Cloud OLAP)}, and \textbf{(4) Rule-Based Routing} across Execution Time ($T_{\pi}$), Routing Accuracy ($\text{Acc}_{\text{top1}}$, $\text{Acc}_{\text{top2}}$, $\le 10\%$ near-optimal hit rate, and zero-regret hit rate), Resource Cost, and Normalized Performance Regret ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}}$, including Mean, Median P50, P90, and P95 Tail Regret).

\subsection{Methodological Rigor: CSCI Non-Circularity, GPU/CPU Operator Mapping, Result Equivalence, and Fairness Protocol}
To guarantee experimental trustworthiness and eliminate confounding across heterogeneous backends, we enforced and audited three methodological protocols (with full statistical and environment manifests in Additional File~1, Tables~S6 and~S10):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{CSCI Physical Derivation, Non-Circularity Distinction, and Weight Perturbation Sensitivity:} The operator weights in Eq.~(1) ($w = [1.5, 2.5, 3.0, 3.5, 4.0, 1.5]$) reflect the normalized memory-pass and time-complexity hierarchy of relational operators relative to a sequential scan ($1.0\times$ pass): linear $\mathcal{O}(N)$ pipeline traversal and branch masking ($D, C = 1.5$), hash-table build/probe and staged CTE materialization ($J = 2.5, S = 3.0$), $\mathcal{O}(N \log N)$ partition sorting in window frames ($W = 3.5$), and super-linear list offset expansion during lateral \texttt{UNNEST} ($U = 4.0$). Crucially, because workload tiers L1--L5 were stratified by operator family, inter-tier Kruskal--Wallis separation of $\text{CSCI}(q)$ ($H = 51.69, p < 0.001$) and unadjusted correlation with AST node count ($\rho = 0.8744, p < 0.001$) represent \emph{structural design verification checks by construction} rather than independent empirical findings. Instead, the non-circular empirical utility of $\text{CSCI}(q)$ is established by three independent proofs: (i) \emph{Within-Tier Partial Correlation}: when simultaneously controlling for both workload tier (L1--L5) and predicate selectivity $\hat{\sigma}(q)$, $\text{CSCI}(q)$ retains a statistically significant within-tier partial correlation with GPU speedup over CPU ($\rho_{\text{partial}}(\text{CSCI}, S_{\text{GPU/CPU}} \mid \text{Tier}, \hat{\sigma}(q)) = 0.2653, \text{df}=71, p = 0.0233$; and $\rho_{\text{partial}} = 0.5477, p < 0.001$ controlling for $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ alone); (ii) \emph{Oblique Tree-Split Compression (M3 vs.\ M2)}: adding $\text{CSCI}(q)$ to the 8D raw AST + selectivity vector in Table~\ref{tab:jbd_csci_oblique_t9b} prunes $-28.2\%$ of axis-aligned depth-3 GBDT splits ($6.84 \rightarrow 4.91$ splits/tree), reduces out-of-fold routing regret by $-9.22\%$ ($p = 0.0038$), and matches 25-fold Ridge-NNLS learned weights ($\hat{\mathbf{w}}_{\text{NNLS}} = [1.46, 2.58, 2.94, 3.56, 4.02, 1.44]$, $\cos(\mathbf{w},\hat{\mathbf{w}})=0.9994$, $p=0.8924$ between M3 and M4); and (iii) \emph{External Transfer}: $\text{CSCI}(q)$ generalizes zero-shot to $N_{\text{ext}}=116$ external queries across five benchmarks not constructed using $\text{CSCI}$ (Official TPC-H Q1--Q22 partial $\rho = 0.5840, p = 0.0054$; TPC-DS-derived Top-1 $+6.66\text{ pp}$ and BIRD-inspired Top-1 $+45.00\text{ pp}$ in M3 vs.\ M2). A 10,000-trial Monte Carlo weight perturbation audit (Additional File~1, Table~S13) confirms $98.96\%$ ($74.2/75$) and $97.84\%$ ($73.4/75$) identical routing decisions at $\pm 10\%$ and $\pm 20\%$ weight perturbations ($\bar{\rho} = 0.9963$ and $0.9880$).
    \item \textbf{GPU vs.\ CPU Architectural Fairness Protocol and Three-Level Timing Equivalence:} Because our PyTorch CUDA relational tensor backend executes six compiled tensor primitives (\texttt{torch.where} filtering, INT64 radix hash-probing and \texttt{index\_select}, \texttt{scatter\_add\_} segmented group-by, \texttt{torch.sort} bitonic/radix sort, \texttt{torch.cumsum} window framing, and \texttt{repeat\_interleave} array unnesting) in 64-bit IEEE~754 (\texttt{FP64}/\texttt{INT64}) precision rather than operating as a standalone SQL DBMS, Table~\ref{tab:gpu_pcie_decomposition} strictly separates three timing boundaries: \textbf{Level~1 (Kernel-to-Kernel)} ($22.25\text{ ms}$ GPU Warm vs.\ $182.87\text{ ms}$ DuckDB compute kernel, $8.22\times$), \textbf{Level~2 (In-Memory Data-in to Result-out)} ($T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}} = 56.96\text{ ms}$ including $33.89\text{ ms}$ pinned PCIe Gen4 H2D + $0.82\text{ ms}$ D2H transfer vs.\ $183.74\text{ ms}$ DuckDB Native, $3.23\times$), and \textbf{Level~3 (Full End-to-End SQL-to-Result)} charging the GPU pipeline with full \texttt{SQLGlot} SQL parsing, AST-to-tensor lowering, and router dispatch ($+4.15\text{ ms}$, yielding $61.11\text{ ms}$ in-memory end-to-end and $131.79\text{ ms}$ from cold on-disk ZSTD Parquet vs.\ $254.42\text{ ms}$ on DuckDB Parquet).
    \item \textbf{Benchmark Fairness Protocol, Stage-Wise Latency Conservation, and Native-SQL vs.\ Tensor Attribution:} Across all $213$ benchmark queries ($228$ including Seed~\texttt{S6}), we verify exact multiset result equivalence ($\text{EX}=1.000$ across all $213/213$ queries and six engines, max \texttt{FP64} relative error $\epsilon_{\text{rel}} \le 5.74\times 10^{-16} < 10^{-9}$) and decompose total latency into seven additive stages (Additional File~1 (Table~S17)):
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:v9_seven_stage_conservation}
        T_{\text{total},e}(q) = \tau_{\text{parse},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{meta},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{io\_load},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{ser\_cast},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{transfer},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{exec},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{mat},e}(q)
    \end{equation}
    Decomposing the total latency reduction over the fastest single CPU SQL engine ($\bar{T}_{e^*_{\text{CPU}}} = 82.47\text{ ms}$, Polars) into pure Native-SQL routing ($\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL}} = \{\text{DuckDB},\text{Polars},\text{DataFusion},\text{BigQuery},\text{SQLite3}\}$, $M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$) and marginal GPU tensor offloading yields:
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:v9_sql_vs_tensor_attribution}
        \Delta \bar{T}_{\text{total}} = \bar{T}_{e^*_{\text{CPU}}} - \bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{all}})} = \underbrace{\big(\bar{T}_{e^*_{\text{CPU}}} - \bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL}})}\big)}_{\Delta_{\text{SQL-Only}} = 13.35\text{ ms}\ (48.5\%)} + \underbrace{\big(\bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL}})} - \bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{all}})}\big)}_{\Delta_{\text{Tensor-Offload}} = 14.20\text{ ms}\ (51.5\%)} = 27.55\text{ ms}
    \end{equation}
    confirming that $48.5\%$ of the latency reduction over the best single CPU engine---and a $2.77\times$ reduction in P95 tail regret ($118.50\% \rightarrow 42.80\%$)---is achieved purely by routing among standard SQL engines with zero tensor operations.
\end{enumerate}
\section{Experimental Validation and Empirical Analysis}

\par\vspace{5pt}\noindent\textbf{Evaluation Organization.} Sections~4.1--4.3 first establish the physical storage, serverless slot-pruning, PCIe transfer ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$), SIMD vectorization, and device-energy crossover laws that explain why static single-engine assignment fails and parameterize Layer~2's analytical guards; Sections~4.4--4.6 then evaluate AST-Route's cost-prediction accuracy, conformal tail-risk control, negative controls, minimal engine subsets, overhead break-even, cross-schema generalization across all $213$ queries ($7$ suites), and $\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$ scaling.

\subsection{Storage Layout, Serverless Cloud FinOps Pruning, and PCIe vs.\ SIMT Crossover Laws}
Across all $75$ core TPC-H queries ($N=375$ timed trials at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$; granular per-tier breakdowns in Additional File~1, Tables~S1--S3 and Figs.~S1--S2), native in-memory columnar storage in DuckDB reduces mean latency by $1.38\times$ from $254.42\text{ ms}$ (external ZSTD Parquet, 95\% CI: $[163.65, 362.54]\text{ ms}$) to $183.74\text{ ms}$ ($[110.71, 274.89]\text{ ms}$, $p < 0.001$, Cliff's $\delta = +0.296$) by eliminating repeated ZSTD decompression. Conversely, Google BigQuery exhibits a serverless scheduling floor of $745.39\text{ ms}$ server job wall-clock time ($48.20\text{ ms}$ slot queue $+ 697.19\text{ ms}$ slot execution; $1124.79\text{ ms}$ client WAN wall-clock, reduced by $1.90\times$ from $1412.60\text{ ms}$ unoptimized server latency, $p < 0.001$, Cliff's $\delta = +0.471$) while allocating $4491.3\text{ ms}$ of parallel slot compute ($\mathcal{M}_{\text{slot}} = 6.37\times$). Under high-selectivity V1 predicates ($\sim 1\text{--}5\%$ selectivity), BigQuery clustering keys prune $78.4\%$ of scanned bytes ($347.7\text{ MB} \rightarrow 75.1\text{ MB}$ at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$), translating into on-demand FinOps savings ($\Delta C_{\text{1M}}$ at $\$6.25/\text{TiB}$, Additional File~1, Table~S3) of $\$1{,}441.61$ ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) to $\$144{,}160.82$ ($\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$) per $10^6$ queries.

Table~\ref{tab:gpu_pcie_decomposition} and Fig.~\ref{fig:gpu_pcie_decomposition} present the three-level CUDA execution telemetry on the NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2050 GPU ($4.0\text{ GB}$ VRAM):
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Break-Even Crossover Analysis of Tier L1 Scans:} In L1 scan-and-filter workloads ($\text{CSCI}=15.3$), warm in-VRAM GPU kernel execution requires $24.46\text{ ms}$ ($\Theta_{\text{eff}} = 13.28\text{ GB/s}$), whereas PCIe Host-to-Device transfer incurs $30.56\text{ ms}$ ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}} = 55.5\%$). By Eq.~(3), the required break-even kernel speedup is $(1 - 0.555)^{-1} = \mathbf{2.25\times}$. Because L1's low arithmetic intensity yields a warm speedup of only $2.05\times < 2.25\times$, cold-start GPU execution ($55.02\text{ ms}$) is slower than DuckDB ($50.84\text{ ms}$), Polars ($38.00\text{ ms}$), and DataFusion ($33.58\text{ ms}$).
    \item \textbf{Non-Linear GPU Speedup in High-CSCI Workloads (L2--L5):} As operator complexity increases ($\text{CSCI} \in [26.8, 37.1]$), cold-start GPU execution ($56.96\text{ ms}$ overall, 95\% CI: $[46.23, 68.44]\text{ ms}$, $p < 0.001$ vs.\ DuckDB) outperforms DuckDB Native CPU by $\mathbf{2.52\times}$ in L2 joins ($104.88$ vs.\ $264.53\text{ ms}$), $\mathbf{2.09\times}$ in L3 CTEs ($58.24$ vs.\ $121.92\text{ ms}$), $\mathbf{4.03\times}$ in L4 windows ($19.37$ vs.\ $78.16\text{ ms}$), and $\mathbf{8.52\times}$ in L5 array unnesting ($47.31$ vs.\ $403.27\text{ ms}$). Even under conservative Level~3 accounting ($+4.15\text{ ms}$ SQL-to-tensor lowering charged to GPU), in-memory GPU execution ($61.11\text{ ms}$) outperforms Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$) by $1.35\times$ and DuckDB ($183.74\text{ ms}$) by $3.01\times$.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Six-Software Cross-Paradigm Synthesis and Energy-Aware Telemetry Scope}
Table~\ref{tab:six_software_matrix} synthesizes execution latency across all six software engines at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ (effective scan throughput $\Theta_{\text{eff}}$ and device energy tables are detailed in Additional File~1, Tables~S4--S5 and Figs.~S3--S5). A seed-clustered Friedman test across the $K=25$ independent seeds confirms highly significant differentiation across the six engines ($\chi^2_F(5) = 93.43, p < 0.001$, Kendall's $W = 0.7475$; query-level $\chi^2_F(5) = 321.50, p < 0.001, W = 0.8573$): (i) vectorized CPU engines achieve complete stochastic dominance over B-tree row-store execution in SQLite~3 ($8417.33\text{ ms}$, Cliff's $\delta = +1.000, p < 0.001$), with mean speedups of $45.8\times$ in DuckDB ($183.74\text{ ms}$), $87.4\times$ in DataFusion ($96.30\text{ ms}$), and $102.1\times$ in Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$); (ii) across CPU paradigms, we observe a \emph{bidirectional architectural crossover}: DuckDB leads all CPU engines by $1.88\times\text{--}3.52\times$ on the $22$ official TPC-H queries ($22.82\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $42.79\text{ ms}$ Polars and $80.41\text{ ms}$ DataFusion in Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part~C) due to cost-based join ordering and correlated subquery decorrelation in Q16--Q22, whereas Polars and DataFusion achieve lower mean latency on the $75$-query L1--L5 factorial suite ($82.47$ and $96.30\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $183.74\text{ ms}$) specifically because Tier~L5 constructs in-query nested lists via \texttt{ARRAY\_AGG}+\texttt{UNNEST} ($154.76$ and $110.31\text{ ms}$ over contiguous Arrow \texttt{ListArray} offset buffers vs.\ $403.27\text{ ms}$ in DuckDB) and Tier~L2 stresses 6-to-8-way multi-attribute hash joins ($81.68\text{ ms}$ Polars vs.\ $264.53\text{ ms}$ DuckDB); and (iii) under our four-boundary energy protocol (Table~\ref{tab:v9_mo_energy_ood_repro_synthesis} Part~B and Additional File~1, Section~S2 and Table~S5), measured NVML GPU board energy averages $0.65\text{ J/query}$ cold ($0.25\text{ J}$ warm; $1.58\text{ J}$ composite Host+GPU system energy $E_{\text{sys}}$), compared to TDP-calibrated \emph{Estimated CPU Package Energy} of $3.17\text{ J}$ on Polars, $3.39\text{ J}$ on DataFusion, and $6.40\text{ J}$ on DuckDB ($\text{MACE}=3.09\%\text{--}3.21\%$ against a non-linear CMOS DVFS reference curve $P\propto u^{1.28}$; $+16.39\text{ W}$ non-CPU motherboard offset verified via 45-second ACPI DC battery-shunt telemetry over $2{,}817$ queries).
