\subsection{Head-to-Head Evaluation of AST-Route Against Four Routing Baselines (25-Seed LOSO-CV)}
Table~\ref{tab:routing_evaluation} and Fig.~\ref{fig:ast_query_router} present the comparative evaluation of our Proposed \textbf{AST-Route} Framework (Fig.~\ref{fig:proposed_ast_framework}) against static, heuristic, and guarded/unguarded linear and shallow-tree baselines under 25-seed Leave-One-Seed-Out cross-validation (guaranteeing zero data leakage across variations V1--V3):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Comparison Against Static Single-Engine Baselines (Models 1--3):} Relying on a single static engine incurs severe performance regret and resource penalties across heterogeneous workloads. \textbf{(1) Always DuckDB} achieves $28.00\%$ Top-1 and $49.33\%$ Top-2 accuracy but suffers from a $\mathbf{271.10\%}$ mean performance regret ($928.32\%$ P95 regret, $183.74\text{ ms}$ mean, $731.22\text{ ms}$ P95, $6.397\text{ J}$, $4.71\text{ GB}$ RAM) due to slower multi-way joins (L2) and array unnesting (L5). \textbf{(2) Always GPU (Cold-Start)} achieves $49.33\%$ Top-1 and $60.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy ($56.96\text{ ms}$ mean, $0.644\text{ J}$, $1.30\text{ GB}$ VRAM) but incurs a $\mathbf{60.76\%}$ mean regret and a $\mathbf{257.31\%}$ P95 tail regret ($620.63\%$ worst-case regret) because low-complexity L1 scans and narrow V1 joins are penalized by PCIe Host-to-Device transfer overhead. \textbf{(3) Always BigQuery} incurs a $745.39\text{ ms}$ mean latency ($2794.05\text{ ms}$ P95), a $\mathbf{1709.86\%}$ latency regret on $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ workloads, $15.720\text{ J}$ of analytical slot-TDP proxy energy ($0\text{ J}$ local compute energy), and a cloud FinOps expenditure of $\mathbf{\$1267.66\text{ USD}}$ per million queries.
    \item \textbf{Comparison Against Heuristic, Guarded Linear/Shallow-Tree, Nested-CV, and Canonical AST-Route (Model 4 vs. Model 5):} \textbf{(4) Rule-Based Routing} (fixed operator thresholds on $J, W, U, S$ in $34.07\text{ }\mu\text{s}$) reaches $71.48\text{ ms}$ ($252.40\%$ P95 regret), a \textbf{2-Feature Scanned-Bytes + Join Rule} reaches $54.19\text{ ms}$ exec ($58.34\text{ ms}$ net, $49.80\%$ mean / $184.50\%$ P95 regret), and the 8-dimensional \emph{Syntax-Only AST Router} reaches $78.31\text{ ms}$ ($198.5\%$ P95 regret). Crucially, Table~\ref{tab:routing_evaluation} reports both pure execution time $T_{\text{exec}}$ and net end-to-end latency $T_{\text{net}} = T_{\text{exec}} + T_{\text{router}}$ across unguarded and $+12\%$ margin-guarded baselines: while unguarded \emph{Ridge Linear Cost-Regression} ($49.12\text{ ms}$ exec / $52.77\text{ ms}$ net) and \emph{Shallow Decision Tree $d=2$} ($51.35\text{ ms}$ exec / $55.00\text{ ms}$ net) achieve low raw execution latency, they suffer $\mathbf{196.8\%}$ and $\mathbf{150.9\%}$ P95 tail regret due to unguarded PCIe offloading; conversely, equipping them with the identical $+12\%$ PCIe guard (\textbf{Ridge $+12\%$ Guard}: $51.84\text{ ms}$ exec / $\mathbf{55.49\text{ ms}}$ net, $38.40\%$ mean / $138.50\%$ P95 regret; \textbf{Shallow DT $+12\%$ Guard}: $52.90\text{ ms}$ exec / $\mathbf{56.55\text{ ms}}$ net, $36.90\%$ mean / $128.40\%$ P95 regret) reveals that our 9-dimensional \textbf{Proposed AST-Route ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$)} outperforms both guarded linear and shallow-tree models in \emph{both} net latency and P95 tail regret ($\text{GPU } R^2 = \mathbf{0.875}$, $\text{Polars } R^2 = \mathbf{0.822}$, $\text{Energy } R^2 = \mathbf{0.815}$ in $497.16\text{ }\mu\text{s}$). Specifically, under fully leak-free \textbf{(5b) Nested Inner-Fold CV (modal $\hat{\tau}_{\text{guard}}^{(-k)}=8\%$)}, AST-Route achieves $51.22\text{ ms}$ exec ($\mathbf{55.37\text{ ms}}$ net), $35.80\%$ mean regret, $\mathbf{118.40\%}$ P95 relative regret ($\mathbf{35.90\text{ ms}}$ P95 abs), and formal two-sided $\pm 5.0\text{ ms}$ TOST equivalence to Always GPU ($p_{\text{TOST}}=\mathbf{0.0442} < 0.05$, $p_{\text{non-inf}}=0.0012$, 90\% TOST CI $[-4.78, +1.60]\text{ ms}$). Under \textbf{(5c) Canonical Fixed $\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$}, AST-Route achieves $50.78\text{ ms}$ exec ($\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ net, 95\% CI: $[42.78, 68.50]\text{ ms}$, P95: $129.37\text{ ms}$), $\mathbf{57.33\%}$ Top-1 ($\mathbf{76.00\%}$ Top-2, $\mathbf{65.33\%}$ $\le 10\%$ hit rate), $\mathbf{0.00\%}$ median regret, $\mathbf{34.98\%}$ mean regret, $\mathbf{114.11\%}$ P95 relative regret ($\mathbf{34.51\text{ ms}}$ P95 abs), and one-sided non-inferiority within $+5.0\text{ ms}$ of Always GPU ($p_{\text{non-inf}}=\mathbf{0.00067}$, $\pm 6.0\text{ ms}$ TOST $p_{\text{TOST},\pm 6\text{ms}}=0.0241$; its two-sided $\pm 5.0\text{ ms}$ TOST is $p=0.069$ solely because the lower 90\% CI bound $[-5.23, +1.15]\text{ ms}$ extends past $-5.0\text{ ms}$ in the \emph{faster-than-GPU} direction) while cutting P95 tail regret by $\mathbf{2.25\times}$ ($p_{\text{raw,seed}}=\mathbf{0.0098}$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=\mathbf{0.0196}$, query $p=\mathbf{0.0021}$; $n=24$ divergent-seed $p_{\text{seed}}=\mathbf{0.0042}$) and outperforming Rule-Based Routing ($p_{\text{seed}}=0.0284$), Always Polars by $\mathbf{1.50\times}$ ($p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0171$), and Always DuckDB by $\mathbf{3.35\times}$ ($p_{\text{Holm,seed}} < 0.001$).
    \item \textbf{Interpretable AST+Selectivity Routing Rules, Misroute Taxonomy, and Zero-Duplication Storage Footprint:} Feature importance analysis confirms that \texttt{Total\_\allowbreak{}AST\_\allowbreak{}Nodes} ($\mathbf{19.95\%}$), \texttt{Selectivity} ($\mathbf{19.81\%}$), \texttt{CSCI\_\allowbreak{}Score} ($\mathbf{15.95\%}$), and \texttt{Joins} ($\mathbf{11.01\%}$) govern $66.72\%$ of individual routing decisions (while grouped feature importance attributes $40.19\%$ to atomic relational operators $[J,S,W,U,C]$, $24.05\%$ to tree size $[D,N]$, $19.81\%$ to selectivity $\sigma(q)$, and $15.95\%$ to $\text{CSCI}(q)$; out-of-fold 50-repeat permutation importance $\Delta R^2$ and TreeSHAP mean $|\phi_i|$ similarly rank $\sigma(q)$ [$\Delta R^2=0.214, |\phi|=0.31$], $\text{CSCI}(q)$ [$0.182, 0.27$], $N(q)$ [$0.176, 0.25$], and $J(q)$ [$0.128, 0.19$] as the top four predictors). Specifically, $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ routes $100.0\%$ of L1 scans to CPU engines ($80.0\%$ DuckDB, $13.3\%$ DataFusion, $6.7\%$ Polars), routes narrow/medium L2 joins ($\sigma(q) \le 0.76$) to Polars/DataFusion ($80.0\%$) while offloading full-horizon V3 joins ($\sigma(q) > 0.76$) to $\text{GPU}_{\text{Cold}}$ ($20.0\%$), and offloads $60.0\%$ of L3 CTEs, $100.0\%$ of L4 windows, and $80.0\%$ of L5 \texttt{UNNEST} workloads to the GPU. Decomposing the $32/75$ ($42.67\%$) non-Top-1 decisions under Canonical AST-Route ($43/75 = 57.33\%$ Top-1, $57/75 = 76.00\%$ Top-2) reveals three distinct categories: (i) $14/75$ ($18.67\%$) are \emph{benign Top-2 near-ties} ($\le 15\%$ regret between Polars and DataFusion/GPU), (ii) $12/75$ ($16.00\%$) are \emph{intentional conservative CPU safety abstentions} triggered by Rules U1--U4 where GPU was marginally faster in hindsight, and (iii) only $6/75$ ($8.00\%$; $7/75$ [$9.33\%$] under nested-CV $8\%$, reduced from $15/75$ [$20.00\%$] unguarded) are \emph{residual adverse GPU dispatches} near the $\sigma=0.22$ medium-selectivity boundary. Crucially, operating across multiple local engines incurs \textbf{zero on-disk storage duplication ($\mathbf{0\text{ MB}}$ extra disk footprint)}, because DuckDB, Polars, DataFusion, and our PyArrow-staged GPU backend all query the single canonical ZSTD Parquet dataset on disk and share in-memory Apache Arrow buffers via zero-copy pointers.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Internal Framework Validation: Feature Ablation, Multi-Objective Pareto Trade-off, Noise Robustness, and Overhead Breakdown}
To rigorously validate the internal mechanisms of \textbf{AST-Route}, Tables~\ref{tab:jbd_ablation_t9} and~\ref{tab:jbd_csci_oblique_t9b} and Additional File~1 (Fig.~S6) report six additional verification experiments under 25-seed LOSO-CV:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Feature Group Ablation and Multicollinearity Defense (Exp A):} Using only basic tree size features (\textbf{A1}: $D(q), N(q)$) results in poor routing accuracy ($44.00\%$, $103.94\text{ ms}$, $137.15\%$ regret). Adding individual operator counts without CSCI or selectivity (\textbf{A2}: $7\text{D}$) reduces latency to $61.06\text{ ms}$ ($50.87\%$ regret). Remarkably, the 2-dimensional compact representation combining our proposed scalar index with selectivity (\textbf{A3}: $\text{CSCI}(q) + \sigma(q)$) achieves $\mathbf{56.30\text{ ms}}$ ($54.67\%$ Top-1 accuracy, $39.11\%$ regret, $1.173\text{ J}$), substantially outperforming both the 7D operator-only model (\textbf{A2}) and the 8D Syntax-Only AST model without selectivity (\textbf{A4}: $73.22\text{ ms}$, $65.78\%$ regret). Variance Inflation Factor (VIF) auditing confirms that the six atomic AST operators and $\sigma(q)$ exhibit low mutual collinearity (mean $\text{VIF} = 1.63$, max $\text{VIF} = 1.86 < 5.0$, orthogonalized $\text{CSCI}$ residual $\text{VIF} = 1.84$). Because Gradient Boosting Decision Trees partition features sequentially via non-linear axis-aligned splits, including $\text{CSCI}(q)$ alongside raw operator counts in \textbf{A5 (Full 9D Proposed AST-Route)} provides a pre-computed linear oblique hyperplane that reduces active split nodes per tree by $-28.2\%$ ($6.84 \rightarrow 4.91$ splits/tree) and improves out-of-fold routing to the optimal $\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ latency and $\mathbf{34.98\%}$ regret ($-9.22\%$ paired regret reduction vs.\ raw AST+$\hat{\sigma}(q)$ without CSCI, 95\% CI $[-15.60\%, -3.45\%]$, Wilcoxon $p=0.0038$). We emphasize that mathematically, $\text{CSCI}(q)$ is an exact deterministic linear combination of the six atomic operator counts $[D,J,S,W,U,C]$ (yielding $R^2=1.000$ against the full six-operator basis and therefore introducing zero new linear degrees of freedom); rather, the reported $\text{VIF}_{D,N}=1.84$ specifically measures low collinearity against basic syntax tree size $[D(q), N(q)]$, and $\text{CSCI}(q)$ serves as an \emph{engineered oblique structural representation} that enables axis-aligned gradient-boosted trees to partition multi-operator complexity without deep staircase splits.
    \item \textbf{Constrained 3D Latency--Energy--FinOps Pareto Frontier and Memory Pruning (Exp B):} Varying the normalized objective weights $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma)$ and hardware memory caps ($M_{\max}$) in Eq.~(8) enables principled navigation across latency, energy, memory, and cloud FinOps dimensions (Table~\ref{tab:ablation_robustness} Exp~B). At $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, shifting from pure latency optimization in B1 ($(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,0,0)$: $54.92\text{ ms}$, $1.381\text{ J}$) to B2 ($(0.75, 0.25, 0)$) preserves virtually identical mean latency ($54.99\text{ ms}$) while reducing P95 tail latency to $124.13\text{ ms}$ and cutting energy by $14.4\%$ ($1.182\text{ J}$), whereas B3 ($(0.50, 0.50, 0)$), B4 ($(0.25, 0.75, 0)$), and B5 ($(0, 1, 0)$) progressively compress energy to $1.007\text{ J}$, $0.879\text{ J}$, and $0.748\text{ J}$ ($-45.8\%$). When a strict GPU memory budget of $M_{\text{gpu\_max}} \le 1.28\text{ GB}$ is enforced in B6 ($(0.60, 0.20, 0.20)$), the feasibility filter $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)$ automatically prunes GPU offloading on L2--L4 queries whose predicted VRAM footprint ($1.33\text{ GB}$) exceeds the cap, routing them to DuckDB/Polars to maintain $1.24\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM with zero OOM violations ($64.18\text{ ms}$, $1.842\text{ J}$). Furthermore, at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ where local CPU engines hit the $9.0\text{ GB}$ RAM cap ($3302\text{ MB}$ disk spill), the Cloud-Elastic policy B7 ($(0.80, 0.10, 0.10)$) offloads spill-bound L1/L4 queries to BigQuery to achieve $1142.8\text{ ms}$ ($19.40\%$ regret, $\$507.06/1\text{M}$ queries), whereas the FinOps-Constrained policy B8 ($(0.40, 0.20, 0.40), C_{\max}=\$0$) eliminates cloud expenditure entirely ($\$0.00/1\text{M}$) while sustaining $1326.9\text{ ms}$ via chunked local GPU/CPU execution (with cloud pricing sensitivity sweeps across $\$3.125\text{--}\$12.50\text{/TiB}$ confirming smooth Pareto adaptation from $1118.4\text{ ms}$ to $1245.6\text{ ms}$). 
    \item \textbf{Selectivity Estimation Noise Robustness and Sample Efficiency (Exp C \& Exp D):} Under 50-trial Monte Carlo uniform multiplicative noise injected into test-time selectivity estimates ($\pm 10\%, \pm 25\%,$ and $\pm 50\%$ in \textbf{C2--C4}), \textbf{AST-Route} maintains $52.80\%\text{--}53.79\%$ Top-1 accuracy ($58.19\%\text{--}60.51\%$ near-optimal hit rate) and $75.00\text{--}80.98\text{ ms}$ mean latency, remaining faster than all static CPU baselines even under $\pm 50\%$ selectivity drift. Furthermore, cold-start learning curves (Additional File~1 (Fig.~S6c)) demonstrate that training on just $5$ canonical seeds ($15$ queries) achieves $63.61\text{ ms}$ ($51.38\%$ regret), already surpassing Rule-Based Routing ($71.48\text{ ms}$), and converges to $56.59\text{ ms}$ by $15$ seeds ($45$ queries). Moreover, when zone-map metadata is deliberately masked on $50\%$ of test queries (simulating incomplete catalog statistics on external tables and falling back to default parametric operator priors without executing the query), AST-Route sustains $53.33\%$ Top-1 accuracy and $68.42\text{ ms}$ mean latency---confirming genuine pre-execution operation independent of actual cardinality.
    \item \textbf{End-to-End Router Overhead Breakdown (Exp E):} Across all 75 SQL files, Stage 1 \texttt{SQLGlot} AST parsing averages $3.476\text{ ms}$ ($3475.91\text{ }\mu\text{s}$), Stage 2 9D feature and zone-map extraction averages $0.172\text{ ms}$ ($172.04\text{ }\mu\text{s}$), and Stage 3 Multi-Output GBR inference averages $0.497\text{ ms}$ ($497.16\text{ }\mu\text{s}$), yielding a total end-to-end framework overhead of $\mathbf{4.145\text{ ms}}$ ($7.55\%$ of mean execution time). Because our reported $54.92\text{ ms}$ AST-Route latency already includes this $4.145\text{ ms}$ warm overhead ($50.775\text{ ms}$ engine execution $+ 4.145\text{ ms}$ router overhead; $55.16\text{ ms}$ under cold NVMe footer seek), net end-to-end AST-Route execution significantly outperforms Rule-Based Routing ($71.48\text{ ms}$), Always Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$), Always DataFusion ($96.30\text{ ms}$), and Always DuckDB ($183.74\text{ ms}$, a $3.35\times$ net end-to-end speedup).
\end{enumerate}

\par\noindent{\scriptsize \textbf{Mathematical Role, Statistical Unit ($K=25$ Seed Clusters Primary / $N=75$ Queries Secondary), Calibration Error ($\text{ECE}$), \& Learned Weight Equivalence Note:} Because $\text{CSCI}(q)=1.5D+2.5J+3.0S+3.5W+4.0U+1.5C$ is an exact linear combination of the six structural operators ($R^2=1.000$ on the 6-operator subspace; zero new linear degrees of freedom), it operates as an \textbf{Engineered Oblique Structural Representation} for axis-aligned GBDTs ($d_{\max}=3$). Compared to unweighted AST + Selectivity (\textbf{M2}), both Canonical \textbf{M3} and 25-fold Ridge-NNLS Learned \textbf{M4} ($\hat{\mathbf{w}}=[1.46,2.58,2.94,3.56,4.02,1.44]$, $\cos(\mathbf{w},\hat{\mathbf{w}})=0.9994$, out-of-fold $\rho=0.9968$, paired $p=0.8924$ between M3 and M4) eliminate $-27.8\%\text{--}28.2\%$ of redundant staircase splits ($6.84 \rightarrow 4.91\text{--}4.94$ splits/tree), boost Top-1 accuracy from $52.00\% \rightarrow 57.33\%$, cut P95 tail regret from $138.45\% \rightarrow 114.11\%\text{--}114.85\%$, and halve Expected Calibration Error ($\text{ECE}: 8.42\% \rightarrow 4.18\%\text{--}4.31\%$; Brier score $0.236 \rightarrow 0.172\text{--}0.175$).}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Seed-Level (\texorpdfstring{$K=25$}{K=25}) Inference, TOST Equivalence, Paired Difference CIs, Sample-Size Sensitivity, and Standalone CPU-Only Routing}
Repeating non-parametric tests strictly on independent seed means ($K=25$ clusters, $N=75$ queries, $375$ timed trials at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$; full bootstrap CIs and baseline matrix in Additional File~1, Tables~S6--S7 and Fig.~S6) confirms cross-engine stratification ($\chi^2_F(5)=93.43, p < 0.001$, Kendall's $W=0.7475$) and establishes our two designated primary evaluation endpoints with explicit 10,000-iteration paired bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals of the differences ($\Delta = \text{AST-Route} - \text{Baseline}$), formal Two One-Sided Tests (TOST) equivalence bounds, and paired effect sizes: (i) on \textbf{Primary Endpoint~E1 (Tail-Risk Compression \& Latency Equivalence vs.\ Always GPU)}, rather than relying on an unpowered two-sided null $p$-value ($p_{\text{seed}}=0.184$) to claim equivalence, a formal paired TOST analysis against Always GPU ($56.96\text{ ms}$) confirms statistical non-inferiority within $+5.0\text{ ms}$ ($\Delta T = -2.04\text{ ms}$, 90\% TOST CI $[-5.23, +1.15]\text{ ms}$, $t(24)=-3.63, p_{\text{non-inf}}=0.00067$, two-sided equivalence $p_{\text{TOST},\pm 5\text{ms}}=0.069$ and $p_{\text{TOST},\pm 6\text{ms}}=\mathbf{0.0241}$ for canonical $\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$ at $54.92\text{ ms}$; and two-sided TOST equivalence within $\pm 5.0\text{ ms}$ at $\mathbf{p_{\text{TOST},\pm 5\text{ms}}=0.0442}$, 90\% TOST CI $[-4.78, +1.60]\text{ ms} \subset [-5.0, +5.0]\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{non-inf}}=0.0012$ for the nested-CV calibrated guard $\hat{\tau}_{\text{guard}}^{(-k)}=8\%$ at $55.37\text{ ms}$). Concurrently, AST-Route significantly reduces routing regret across the full 75-query workload ($K=25$ seeds: $p_{\text{raw,seed}}=\mathbf{0.0098}, p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=\mathbf{0.0196}$; query-level $N=75$: $p_{\text{raw,query}}=\mathbf{0.0021}$; and on the $n=24/75$ [$K=10$ seeds] differentially dispatched subset: $\Delta T_{\text{disp}} = -6.38\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI $[-11.42, -2.15]\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{seed}}=\mathbf{0.0042}$, $d_z = -0.64$), compressing P95 relative regret by $\mathbf{2.25\times}$ ($\Delta\mathcal{R}_{\text{P95}} = -143.20\text{ pp}$, 95\% CI $[-218.45, -64.12]\text{ pp}$ from $257.31\%$ to $114.11\%$; $118.40\%$ under nested-CV) and P95 absolute regret by $\Delta T_{\text{P95,abs}} = -16.91\text{ ms}$ (95\% CI $[-28.40, -6.85]\text{ ms}$ from $51.42\text{ ms}$ to $34.51\text{ ms}$; $35.90\text{ ms}$ under nested-CV, $d_z = -0.58$); and (ii) on \textbf{Primary Endpoint~E2 (Paired Net Latency Reduction vs.\ Static CPU Baselines)}, AST-Route significantly outperforms Always Polars ($\Delta T = -27.55\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI $[-39.59, -16.85]\text{ ms}$, $1.50\times$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0171$, Cliff's $\delta = -0.420$ [Medium], $d_z = -0.60$), Always DataFusion ($\Delta T = -41.38\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI $[-57.61, -26.76]\text{ ms}$, $1.75\times$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0041$, $\delta = -0.493$ [Large], $d_z = -0.71$), Always DuckDB ($\Delta T = -128.82\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI $[-220.68, -59.02]\text{ ms}$, $3.35\times$, $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=5.45\times 10^{-4}$, $\delta = -0.285$ [Small], $d_z = -0.40$), and Rule-Based Routing ($\Delta T = -16.56\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI $[-28.94, -5.18]\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0284$, $d_z = -0.34$). Evaluating sample-size sensitivity across $K \in \{5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ canonical seeds shows rapid convergence and narrowing 95\% bootstrap CI half-widths: $63.61\text{ ms}$ ($\pm 28.40\text{ ms}$ at $K=5, N=15$) $\rightarrow 60.12\text{ ms}$ ($\pm 21.20\text{ ms}$ at $K=10$) $\rightarrow 56.59\text{ ms}$ ($\pm 16.80\text{ ms}$ at $K=15$) $\rightarrow 55.48\text{ ms}$ ($\pm 14.50\text{ ms}$ at $K=20$) $\rightarrow \mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ ($\pm 12.86\text{ ms}$ at $K=25, N=75$, achieving $86.4\%$ statistical power at $\alpha=0.05$ for $\Delta T$ vs.\ Polars and $89.2\%$ power for $\Delta\mathcal{R}_{\text{P95}}$ vs.\ GPU). Auditing the 25-seed LOSO-CV benchmark under a nested inner 6-fold seed-grouped cross-validation loop selects a modal guard of $\hat{\tau}_{\text{guard}}^{(-k)}=8.0\%$ and yields $55.37\text{ ms}$ net latency ($118.40\%$ P95 relative regret, $35.90\text{ ms}$ P95 absolute regret, $p=0.6486$ vs.\ canonical fixed $\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$ at $54.92\text{ ms}$), beating or tying Always DuckDB on $22/25$ seeds, Always Polars on $20/25$ seeds, and Always GPU on $20/25$ seeds. Furthermore, restricting the candidate pool strictly to native SQL CPU engines ($\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL-Only}}, M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0\text{ GB}$ in Additional File~1, Table~S7 Part~B) achieves $74.67\%$ Top-1 ($56/75$) and $96.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy at $69.12\text{ ms}$ net latency ($9.64\%$ mean regret, $42.80\%$ P95 regret, paired $\Delta T = -13.35\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI $[-22.18, -5.42]\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{Holm}}=0.0084$, $d_z = -0.44$ vs.\ Polars at $82.47\text{ ms}$), confirming substantial routing value on pure native-SQL infrastructures with zero GPU hardware.

\subsubsection{Negative Controls, Minimal Subsets, Per-Engine/Per-Tier Conformal Coverage, Multi-Objective Policies, Energy Agreement, OOD/Concurrency Stress, and R1--R6 Verification}
\label{sec:v9_reviewer_reanalysis}
To evaluate practical systems deployment boundaries and independent reproducibility from our raw execution telemetry (\texttt{reproducibility/verify\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}regenerate\_\allowbreak{}main\_\allowbreak{}tables\_\allowbreak{}R1\_\allowbreak{}R6.py}), Tables~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} and~\ref{tab:v9_mo_energy_ood_repro_synthesis} report seven complementary analyses:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Both Relative (\%) and Absolute (ms) Regret Alongside Negative Controls, Direct Classifiers, and Hedged Top-2 Racing (Table~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} Part~A):} Because relative regret $\mathcal{R}_{\text{rel}}(q) = (T_{\hat{e}}(q) - T_{e^*}(q))/T_{e^*}(q)$ divides by the oracle latency $T_{e^*}(q)$, a $114.11\%$ P95 relative regret on a fast $15\text{--}30\text{ ms}$ Tier~L1 scan corresponds to an \emph{absolute} P95 latency gap of only $\mathbf{34.51\text{ ms}}$ (mean absolute regret $\mathbf{8.14\text{ ms}}$, median $\mathbf{0.00\text{ ms}}$), compared to $\mathbf{51.42\text{ ms}}$ P95 absolute regret on Always GPU, $\mathbf{179.73\text{ ms}}$ on Always Polars, and $\mathbf{656.10\text{ ms}}$ on Always DuckDB. Benchmarking against non-informative negative controls confirms that Uniform Random Routing ($104.98\text{ ms}$ net, $212.16\%$ mean / $561.71\%$ P95 relative regret, $194.28\text{ ms}$ P95 absolute regret, $20.67\%$ Top-1) and 1,000-trial Shuffled-Label Permutation Routing ($95.97\text{ ms}$ net, $183.75\%$ mean / $494.68\%$ P95 relative regret, $175.42\text{ ms}$ P95 absolute regret, $24.13\%$ Top-1) perform nearly $2\times$ worse in net latency and $4.3\times\text{--}4.9\times$ worse in P95 regret than AST-Route ($p_{\text{seed}} < 0.001$). Similarly, a Direct Cost-Sensitive Random Forest Classifier ($61.74\text{ ms}$ net, $186.11\%$ P95 regret, $51.42\text{ ms}$ P95 abs regret) and Histogram-Based GBDT (\texttt{HistGBR-9D}: $61.66\text{ ms}$ net, $192.45\%$ P95 regret) trail multi-output $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ while lacking continuous resource predictions. Under \textbf{Hedged / Racing Top-2 Parallel Execution}, an idealized two-node cluster achieves $45.68\text{ ms}$ net ($19.47\%$ P95 regret), whereas on a single shared workstation concurrent CPU+GPU execution contends for pinned PCIe DMA and DDR4 bandwidth ($1.66\times$ total compute work, $2.49\text{ J}$ vs.\ $1.38\text{ J}$), yielding $54.08\text{ ms}$ net latency ($86.42\%$ P95 regret, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.612$ vs.\ single-dispatch AST-Route at $54.92\text{ ms}$).
    \item \textbf{Leave-One-Engine-Out (LOEO), Minimal 2-Engine / 3-Engine Candidate Subsets Under Realized Routed vs.\ Theoretical Oracle Denominators, and Complexity Justification (Table~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} Part~B):} We report subset retention against both the \emph{realized 6-engine routed latency reduction over Polars} ($\Delta T_{\text{routed}} = 82.47 - 54.92 = 27.55\text{ ms}$) and the \emph{theoretical 6-engine oracle reduction} ($\Delta T_{\text{oracle}} = 82.47 - 42.64 = 39.83\text{ ms}$). Removing SQLite~3 and BigQuery at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ has $0.00\text{ ms}$ impact ($54.92\text{ ms}$, $100.0\%$ routed / $100.0\%$ oracle), removing DataFusion shifts net latency by only $+0.15\text{ ms}$ ($55.07\text{ ms}$), and a minimal \textbf{3-engine subset} ($\{\text{DuckDB}, \text{Polars}, \text{GPU}\}$: $55.07\text{ ms}$ net, $43.09\text{ ms}$ subset oracle) captures $\mathbf{99.5\%}$ ($27.40/27.55\text{ ms}$) of the realized routed gain and $\mathbf{98.9\%}$ ($39.38/39.83\text{ ms}$) of the 6-engine oracle reduction. Crucially, a minimal \textbf{2-engine subset} pairing one vectorized CPU engine with the GPU captures $\mathbf{97.3\%}$ ($\{\text{DuckDB}, \text{GPU}\}$: $55.66\text{ ms}$ net, $26.81/27.55\text{ ms}$; $\mathbf{88.7\%}$ of oracle) to $\mathbf{98.7\%}$ ($\{\text{Polars}, \text{GPU}\}$: $55.28\text{ ms}$ net, $27.19/27.55\text{ ms}$; $\mathbf{87.3\%}$ of oracle) of the realized routed gain, whereas dropping the GPU entirely ($\{\text{DuckDB}, \text{Polars}, \text{DataFusion}\}$: $69.12\text{ ms}$ net) captures $\mathbf{48.5\%}$ ($13.35/27.55\text{ ms}$; $\mathbf{33.5\%}$ of oracle) using pure CPU SQL engines alone. From a systems deployment standpoint, this indicates that \emph{for single-node in-memory workloads ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) with a discrete GPU, a two-engine $\{\text{Polars}, \text{GPU}\}$ or $\{\text{DuckDB}, \text{GPU}\}$ deployment is the complexity-optimal production configuration}, avoiding multi-engine maintenance overhead; conversely, the wider five-active-engine pool becomes indispensable on CPU-only x86\_64 and ARM64 hosts (where routing arbitrates between Polars on selective scans and DuckDB on multi-way joins Q1--Q22) and at out-of-core scale ($\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, where BigQuery absorbs $20.0\%$ of queries under P1 to bypass $6.84\text{ GB}$ local NVMe spills).
    \item \textbf{Stepwise Uncertainty-Aware Abstention Ablation ($\text{U0}\rightarrow\text{U1--U4}$) and Joint Sensitivity Across $\tau_{\text{guard}} \in [0\%, 30\%]$ and $\alpha_{\text{conf}} \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20\}$ (Table~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} Part~C):} Decomposing our uncertainty-aware abstention stack stepwise from \textbf{U0 (Unguarded $\arg\min$, $\tau_{\text{guard}}=0\%$)} ($53.33\%$ Top-1, $58.64\text{ ms}$ net, $48.12\%$ mean / $164.80\%$ P95 relative regret [$49.12\text{ ms}$ P95 abs], $14.67\%$ severe $>100\%$ failures) confirms that \textbf{U1/U2 (Conformal Upper-Bound Gate Only)} reduces net latency to $55.48\text{ ms}$ ($124.60\%$ P95 regret, $8.00\%$ severe failures), \textbf{U3 ($12\%$ Relative Margin Only)} reaches $55.21\text{ ms}$ ($132.40\%$ P95 regret, $9.33\%$ severe failures), and \textbf{U1--U4 (Full Proposed Guard + Conformal + String/Memory Gates)} achieves the optimal $\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ net latency, $\mathbf{114.11\%}$ P95 relative regret ($\mathbf{34.51\text{ ms}}$ P95 abs), and $\mathbf{6.67\%}$ severe failures. Sweeping $\tau_{\text{guard}}$ across $[0\%, 30\%]$ demonstrates a smooth, convex trade-off across $4\%$ ($57.12\text{ ms}$, $142.30\%$ P95), $\mathbf{8\%}$ ($\mathbf{55.37\text{ ms}}$, $\mathbf{118.40\%}$ \textbf{P95}, $\mathbf{35.90\text{ ms}}$ \textbf{P95 abs}, nested inner-CV modal selection), $\mathbf{12\%}$ ($\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$, $\mathbf{114.11\%}$ \textbf{P95}, $\mathbf{34.51\text{ ms}}$ \textbf{P95 abs}), and $15\%$ ($55.18\text{ ms}$, $114.11\%$ P95) before over-abstaining to CPU at $\tau_{\text{guard}}\in[20\%,30\%]$ ($56.85\text{--}60.42\text{ ms}$). Orthogonal sensitivity testing across $\alpha_{\text{conf}} \in \{0.05\ [95\%\text{ PI}],\ 0.10\ [90\%\text{ PI}],\ 0.15\ [85\%\text{ PI}],\ 0.20\ [80\%\text{ PI}]\}$ confirms calibrated empirical coverage ($94.67\% \rightarrow \mathbf{90.67\%} \rightarrow 85.33\% \rightarrow 81.33\%$) and stable routing latency ($55.64\text{ ms}$ [$112.4\%$ P95] $\rightarrow \mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ [$\mathbf{114.11\%}$ \textbf{P95}] $\rightarrow 55.41\text{ ms}$ [$128.5\%$ P95] $\rightarrow 56.82\text{ ms}$ [$146.2\%$ P95]).
    \item \textbf{Per-Engine and Per-Tier ($L1\text{--}L5$) Out-of-Fold $90\%$ Conformal Coverage with Wilson 95\% CIs, Pinball Quantile Comparison, and Exchangeability Boundary (Table~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} Part~D):} Computing conformal quantiles strictly on out-of-fold calibration residuals with finite-sample correction $\lceil(n_{\text{cal}}+1)(1-\alpha_{\text{conf}})\rceil/n_{\text{cal}}$ ($n_{\text{cal}}=72$/fold, $N=75$) achieves $\mathbf{90.67\%}$ empirical coverage ($68/75$ queries, Wilson 95\% CI $\mathbf{[81.97\%, 95.41\%]}$, $\text{ECE}=1.78\%$) under Standard Split Conformal and $\mathbf{90.93\%}$ ($\mathbf{[82.31\%, 95.58\%]}$, $\text{ECE}=\mathbf{0.67\%}$) under Complexity-Weighted Mondrian Conformal across all five active engines: DuckDB ($90.67\%$, half-width $\pm 321.6\text{ ms}$ Split / $\pm 224.8\text{ ms}$ Mondrian), Polars ($90.67\%$, $\pm 50.3\text{ ms}$ / $\pm 44.3\text{ ms}$), DataFusion ($90.67\%$, $\pm 61.4\text{ ms}$ / $\pm 51.0\text{ ms}$), GPU Cold ($90.67\%$, $\pm 15.0\text{ ms}$ / $\pm 14.8\text{ ms}$), and BigQuery ($90.67\%$, $\pm 695.3\text{ ms}$ / $\pm 582.3\text{ ms}$). Benchmarking against direct \textbf{Pinball-Loss Quantile Regression (\texttt{GBR-Quantile}, $\alpha=0.90$)} without conformal calibration confirms that uncalibrated quantile trees under-cover at $82.67\%$ ($62/75$, Wilson 95\% CI $[72.58\%, 89.58\%]$, $\text{ECE}=7.33\%$, $56.18\text{ ms}$ net, $134.20\%$ P95 regret vs.\ $114.11\%$ conformal). Crucially, breaking down coverage across tiers L1--L5 ($n=15$/tier) reveals that Standard Split Conformal over-covers low-variance L1 ($96.00\%$, $\pm 14.2\text{ ms}$) and L3 ($100.00\%$, $\pm 15.6\text{ ms}$) while under-covering high-variance Tier~L5 \texttt{UNNEST} ($81.33\%$ $[56.00\%, 93.68\%]$, $\pm 14.8\text{ ms}$; L2 $89.33\%$, L4 $86.67\%$), whereas \textbf{Complexity-Weighted Mondrian Conformal} equalizes tier-conditional coverage across all five tiers: L1 $\mathbf{90.67\%}$ ($\pm 9.8\text{ ms}$), L2 $\mathbf{89.33\%}$ ($\pm 21.4\text{ ms}$), L3 $\mathbf{90.67\%}$ ($\pm 14.1\text{ ms}$), L4 $\mathbf{90.67\%}$ ($\pm 6.4\text{ ms}$), and L5 $\mathbf{93.33\%}$ ($[70.18\%, 98.81\%]$, $\pm 17.8\text{ ms}$). We explicitly note that the $90\%$ conformal guarantee relies on \emph{exchangeability}: when exchangeability is broken under 5-fold Leave-One-Tier-Out CV (unseen SQL operator class, $N=75$) or 9D string-skewed IMDB JOB ($N=33$), coverage drops to $34.67\%$ ($[24.89\%, 45.95\%]$) and $63.64\%$ ($[46.62\%, 77.81\%]$), respectively---proving why conformal bounds must be paired with our structural OOD fallback gate ($76.18\text{ ms}$) and 12D catalog sketches + Mondrian stratification (which restore external coverage to $\mathbf{89.39\%\text{--}91.89\%}$ across JOB 12D $[74.53\%, 96.04\%]$, SSB+ClickBench $[76.43\%, 96.86\%]$, TPC-DS-derived $[75.09\%, 96.72\%]$, and BIRD-inspired $[71.69\%, 97.67\%]$).
    \item \textbf{Three Additive Router Overhead Regimes, Break-Even Latency, and Unweighted vs.\ Scan/Join-Skewed Production Mix Savings (Table~\ref{tab:v9_reviewer_reanalysis_matrix} Part~E):} We distinguish three exact, additive pre-execution router overhead regimes: (i) \emph{Regime O1 (Cold SQL Parse + Cached Zone-Map + Live 5-Engine GBR Inference)}, totaling $3.476\text{ ms}$ \texttt{SQLGlot} parse $+ 0.172\text{ ms}$ cached zone-map $+ 0.497\text{ ms}$ 5-engine GBR \texttt{.predict()} $= \mathbf{4.15\text{ ms}}$ ($4.145\text{ ms}$, break-even query runtime $T_{\text{BE}}=4.145/0.280=\mathbf{14.80\text{ ms}}$; or $3.476 + 0.413 + 0.497 = \mathbf{4.39\text{ ms}}$ [$4.386\text{ ms}$, $T_{\text{BE}}=\mathbf{15.66\text{ ms}}$] with cold NVMe \texttt{seek(-8)} footer reads); (ii) \emph{Regime O2 (AST-Only Template Cache + Live Zone-Map + Live 5-Engine GBR Inference)}, where caching the normalized SQL template's 8D static AST vector in an FNV-1a hash table ($0.005\text{ ms}$ [$5.2\,\mu\text{s}$]) eliminates the $3.476\text{ ms}$ \texttt{SQLGlot} parse while still running live Python zone-map extraction ($0.172\text{ ms}$) and live 5-engine GBR inference ($0.497\text{ ms}$), totaling $0.005 + 0.172 + 0.497 = \mathbf{0.67\text{ ms}}$ ($674.4\,\mu\text{s}$, break-even $\mathbf{T_{\text{BE}}=2.41\text{ ms}}$); and (iii) \emph{Regime O3 (Pre-Compiled 1D Selectivity Threshold Cache)}, where because all 8 static AST features $[D,N,J,S,W,U,C,\text{CSCI}]$ are fixed for a cached template fingerprint, the 5-engine GBDT decision boundary reduces to a 1D step function of $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ alone---combining FNV-1a template hash lookup ($0.005\text{ ms}$ [$5.2\,\mu\text{s}$]), contiguous C-array min/max interpolation ($0.025\text{ ms}$ [$25.0\,\mu\text{s}$]), and pre-compiled 1D threshold binary search ($0.051\text{ ms}$ [$51.2\,\mu\text{s}$]) to total $\mathbf{0.08\text{ ms}}$ ($81.4\,\mu\text{s}$, break-even $\mathbf{T_{\text{BE}}=0.29\text{ ms}}$). Across the unweighted 75-query suite, AST-Route saves $\mathbf{27.55\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ queries ($31.62\text{ s}/1\text{k}$ cached) vs.\ Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$) at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, and at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($1{,}142.80\text{ ms}$) saves $\mathbf{56.80\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ vs.\ \emph{Best Single Engine Overall} (\texttt{Always GPU}, $1{,}199.60\text{ ms}$) and $\mathbf{5{,}408.62\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ ($\mathbf{1.50\text{ hr}}$) vs.\ \emph{Best Single CPU Engine} (\texttt{Always Polars}, $6{,}551.42\text{ ms}$). Furthermore, because the Amazon Redshift fleet trace (\textbf{Redset}~\cite{vanrenen2024redset}) logs scan, join, and aggregation counts rather than a dedicated \texttt{UNNEST} column, we evaluate a scan/join-skewed production mix sensitivity analysis ($w = [0.42, 0.28, 0.15, 0.10, 0.05]$ across L1--L5, assigning a $5\%$ sensitivity weight to L5 array queries): under this skewed mix, AST-Route ($53.78\text{ ms}$ cold / $49.71\text{ ms}$ cached at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$; $1{,}241.89\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$) saves $\mathbf{23.99\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ ($\mathbf{28.06\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ cached) vs.\ Polars ($77.77\text{ ms}$) at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, and at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ saves $\mathbf{45.46\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ vs.\ Always GPU ($1{,}287.35\text{ ms}$) and $\mathbf{4{,}974.23\text{ s}/1\text{k}}$ ($\mathbf{1.38\text{ hr}}$) vs.\ Always Polars ($6{,}216.12\text{ ms}$).
    \item \textbf{Multi-Objective Weight Sensitivity Across Policies P1--P4 and 4-Boundary Energy Validation Across Always CPU, Always GPU, and AST-Route (Table~\ref{tab:v9_mo_energy_ood_repro_synthesis} Parts~A--B):} Evaluating four canonical multi-objective policies---\textbf{P1: Latency-First $(1,0,0)$}, \textbf{P2: Energy-First $(0.1,0.8,0.1)$}, \textbf{P3: Cost-First / FinOps Zero-Cloud $(0.2,0.1,0.7)$}, and \textbf{P4: Balanced $(0.5,0.3,0.2)$}---demonstrates how engine selection shifts smoothly from $68.0\%$ GPU / $16.0\%$ DuckDB / $13.3\%$ Polars / $2.7\%$ DataFusion under P1 ($54.92\text{ ms}$, $1.381\text{ J/q}$ [$1.38\text{ kJ}/1\text{k}$], $0.790\text{ J}$ dynamic) to $88.0\%$ GPU / $6.7\%$ Polars / $5.3\%$ DuckDB under P2 ($61.45\text{ ms}$, $0.812\text{ J/q}$ [$0.81\text{ kJ}/1\text{k}$], $0.395\text{ J}$ dynamic, $-41.2\%$ energy vs.\ P1 and $-74.4\%$ vs.\ Always Polars at $3.170\text{ J/q}$ [$3.17\text{ kJ}/1\text{k}$] / $-87.3\%$ vs.\ Always DuckDB at $6.397\text{ J/q}$ [$6.40\text{ kJ}/1\text{k}$]) at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, and from $20.0\%$ BigQuery cloud spill offload under P1 at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($1142.80\text{ ms}$, $\$507.06/1\text{M}$) to $12.0\%$ BigQuery under P4 ($1168.50\text{ ms}$, $\$304.24/1\text{M}$, highest 3D Pareto hypervolume $0.9340$) and $0.0\%$ BigQuery under P3 ($1326.90\text{ ms}$, $\$0.00/1\text{M}$). Part~B synthesizes our 4-boundary energy calibration ($R_{\text{cal}}=50$ repetitions/tier, $250$ runs), reporting idle/active/peak power, $\text{J/query}$, and $\text{kJ}/1\text{k}$ queries across \texttt{Always GPU} ($4.80\text{ W}$ idle, $11.31\text{ W}$ avg, $28.4\text{ W}$ peak, $0.644\text{ J/q}$ [$0.64\text{ kJ}/1\text{k}$] board; $19.30\text{ W}$ idle, $27.74\text{ W}$ avg, $1.580\text{ J/q}$ [$1.58\text{ kJ}/1\text{k}$] Host+GPU composite), \texttt{Always CPU} ($14.50\text{ W}$ idle, $34.82\text{--}38.41\text{ W}$ avg, $44.2\text{--}44.8\text{ W}$ peak, $3.170\text{--}6.397\text{ J/q}$), and \textbf{Mainboard ACPI DC Battery-Rail Physical Shunt Telemetry} ($2{,}817$ sustained queries over $45\text{ s}$ with AC brick unplugged and display panel off: $20.97\text{ W}$ idle $\rightarrow 46.40\text{ W}$ active, $0.792\text{ J/q}$ [$0.79\text{ kJ}/1\text{k}$] gross DC vs.\ $0.479\text{ J/q}$ CPU TDP estimate, isolating the $+16.39\text{ W}$ [$+35.3\%$] non-CPU DRAM/NVMe/VRM platform rail offset with rank concordance $\rho=0.9945$), alongside NVML sensor agreement ($\text{MAE}=0.008\text{ J}$, $\text{RMSE}=0.010\text{ J}$, $\text{MACE}=1.24\%$, 95\% LoA $[-0.018,+0.020]\text{ J}$) and TDP-vs.-DVFS CPU agreement ($\text{MAE}=0.098\text{--}0.205\text{ J}$, $\text{RMSE}=0.102\text{--}0.214\text{ J}$, $\text{MACE}=3.09\%\text{--}3.21\%$, 95\% LoA $[-0.201,+0.197]\text{ J}$ Polars / $[-0.422,+0.414]\text{ J}$ DuckDB).
    \item \textbf{OOD / Dynamic Workload Drift Stress Test ($\text{D0}\rightarrow\text{D4}$), $C=1\text{--}64$ Concurrency Saturation, and One-Command R1--R6 Reproducibility Audit (Table~\ref{tab:v9_mo_energy_ood_repro_synthesis} Parts~C--D):} Under Out-of-Distribution (OOD) and non-stationary drift stress (Part~C), AST-Route's structural and string/memory gates achieve $\mathbf{92.0\%\text{--}100.0\%}$ \textbf{OOD detection recall} with only $0.0\%\text{--}9.1\%$ false abstentions, recovering unseen-operator LOTO-CV latency from $85.42\text{ ms}$ to $76.18\text{ ms}$ ($p_{\text{seed}}=0.0312$ vs.\ Polars). Across the 5-stage dynamic drift protocol ($\text{D0}$ Stationary [$90.7\%$ cov, $54.92\text{ ms}$] $\rightarrow \text{D1}$ Scan-to-Join/Window Mix Shift $20\%\rightarrow 80\%$ [$89.3\%$ cov, $61.20\text{ ms}$, $0\text{ ms}$ retraining] $\rightarrow \text{D2a/b}$ Selectivity Shift [$\text{PSI}=0.38>0.25$ triggers a $0.413\text{ ms}$ Parquet footer refresh, cutting latency from $68.85\rightarrow 64.49\text{ ms}$ and restoring $90.7\%$ cov] $\rightarrow \text{D3}$ Moderate Skew $s=0.8$ [$88.0\%$ cov, $65.80\text{ ms}$] $\rightarrow \text{D4a/b}$ Severe Zipfian Skew $s=1.4$ [$\text{PI}=75.0\% < 80\%$ triggers a 5-canary residual recalibration in $0.40\text{ s}$ [$412.4\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $9.2\text{ s}$ full retraining], restoring coverage to $89.0\%$ and cutting latency from $84.21\rightarrow 76.13\text{ ms}$]), drift monitoring incurs $0.0\%$ false alarms. Extending concurrent stream testing from $C=1\text{--}32$ to $C=64$ pinpoints the exact hardware saturation boundary: throughput peaks at $C=32$ ($\mathbf{154.44\text{ QPS}}$, $34.8\text{ ms}$ queue, $207.2\text{ ms}$ mean, $2.14\times$ over static CPU) before pinned PCIe Gen4 DMA and host DDR4 memory channels saturate at $C=64$ ($146.00\text{ QPS}$, $68.4\text{ ms}$ queue, $438.2\text{ ms}$ mean vs.\ $68.12\text{ QPS}$ and $939.5\text{ ms}$ on static CPU). Finally, Part~D summarizes our single-command verification harness (\texttt{python reproducibility/verify\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}regenerate\_\allowbreak{}main\_\allowbreak{}tables\_\allowbreak{}R1\_\allowbreak{}R6.py}), which re-verifies all six reproducibility protocols (\textbf{R1--R6}) from raw CSV logs with $0.000$ maximum discrepancy across all manuscript tables.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Cost Prediction Q-Error, Regret Distribution, Tier-by-Tier Rule Comparison, and Unseen Seed Generalization}
Table~\ref{tab:priority3_validation} and Fig.~\ref{fig:priority3_unseen_cost} present the out-of-sample validation experiments examining cost-model precision, regret quantiles, tier-level behavior against heuristics, and generalization to strictly unseen query seeds:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Multi-Output Cost and Resource Prediction Accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:priority3_validation}, Part~A):} Evaluated across all 75 queries out-of-fold, Layer 3 achieves high predictive fidelity across the primary local and GPU execution targets: on CUDA GPU cold-start latency, the model achieves $\log\text{-}R^2 = \mathbf{0.875}$, $\text{MAPE} = \mathbf{13.34\%}$, a median multiplicative Q-Error of $\text{P50} = \mathbf{1.053}$ ($5.3\%$ median deviation from ground truth), and a 90th-percentile Q-Error of $\text{P90} = \mathbf{1.371}$ (mean predicted $57.16\text{ ms}$ vs. true $56.96\text{ ms}$). On Polars and DataFusion, median Q-Errors are $\mathbf{1.095}$ ($\text{MAPE}=16.79\%, R^2=0.822$) and $\mathbf{1.105}$ ($\text{MAPE}=23.82\%, R^2=0.594$), respectively. For per-query energy footprint ($\text{Joules}$) and host working-set memory ($\text{GB}$), median Q-Errors reach $\mathbf{1.172}$ ($R^2=0.815$) and $\mathbf{1.007}$ ($\text{MAPE}=2.21\%, R^2=0.994$). Crucially, while BigQuery exhibits lower point-prediction accuracy ($\text{P50}=1.326, \text{MAPE}=44.18\%, R^2=0.462$) due to multi-tenant cloud slot scheduling jitter, this variance \emph{never degrades cloud fallback routing decisions} for two structural reasons: (a) at $\text{SF}=1\text{--}30\text{ GB}$, BigQuery's latency floor ($745\text{--}1{,}789\text{ ms}$) is separated by $4\times\text{--}14\times$ from local engines ($55\text{--}482\text{ ms}$) and guarded by Rule~U2's $90\%$ conformal upper bound $\hat{U}_{0.90,\text{BQ}}(q)$, yielding $\mathbf{0.0\%}$ false-positive cloud dispatches; and (b) at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, cloud fallback under Rule~U4 is governed primarily by the highly accurate \emph{host working-set memory predictor} ($R^2 = \mathbf{0.994}, \text{MAPE} = \mathbf{2.21\%}, \text{Q-Error P50} = \mathbf{1.007}$), which detects when intermediate hash-join partitions exceed the $9.0\text{ GB}$ RAM cap ($6{,}840\text{ MB}$ NVMe spill at $6{,}551\text{--}8{,}700\text{ ms}$ on CPU vs.\ $2{,}571.60\text{ ms}$ on BigQuery) with $\mathbf{100.0\%}$ cloud-spill trigger precision and recall. To explicitly guard against cost-prediction uncertainty across heterogeneous engines (where out-of-fold 90\% conformal prediction intervals achieve $88.0\%\text{--}94.7\%$ empirical coverage with half-widths of $\pm 11.4\text{ ms}$ on GPU, $\pm 14.8\text{ ms}$ on Polars, and $\pm 318.5\text{ ms}$ on BigQuery), AST-Route enforces confidence-aware abstention (Table~\ref{tab:priority3_validation} Part~C): the dispatcher abstains from GPU offloading when the predicted latency advantage over vectorized CPU falls within the $12\%$ PCIe uncertainty margin and abstains from cloud offloading unless BigQuery's upper 90\% prediction bound beats local execution. Evaluating risk-based routing metrics across all 75 queries confirms that when the cost model is uncertain, defaulting to a safer deterministic CPU engine substantially outperforms naive point-estimate $\arg\min$ selection: across the six candidate targets in Part A, the 90\% conformal prediction intervals achieve $88.0\%\text{--}94.7\%$ empirical coverage (mean coverage $90.9\%$, Expected Calibration Error $\text{ECE}=1.78\%$). By abstaining from risky GPU PCIe transfers on $16.00\%$ ($12/75$) of TPC-H queries ($48.48\%$ [$16/33$] total abstentions on JOB: $21.21\%$ [$7/33$] via Rule~U1's $12\%$ margin guard plus $27.27\%$ [$9/33$] via Rule~U3's unanchored \texttt{LIKE} string guard, alongside $51.52\%$ [$17/33$] routed directly to Polars by point-estimate $\arg\min$), risk-based confidence-aware routing reduces the probability of selecting a slower engine ($P_{\text{subopt}} = 1 - \text{Zero-Regret Hit Rate}$) from $46.67\%$ ($35/75$) under point-estimate $\arg\min$ to $41.33\%$ ($31/75$), lowers expected latency regret from $48.12\%$ ($58.64\text{ ms}$) to $34.98\%$ ($54.92\text{ ms}$), cuts P95 tail regret from $164.80\%$ to $114.11\%$, reduces worst-case (maximum) regret from $312.40\%$ to $245.18\%$ (vs.\ $620.63\%$ on Always GPU), more than halves the high-regret failure rate ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}} > 100\%$) from $14.67\%$ ($11/75$) to $6.67\%$ ($5/75$), and elevates routing decision stability across repeated trials from $93.33\%$ to $98.67\%$.
    \item \textbf{Generalization to Strictly Unseen Query Seeds and External Cross-Schema Workloads (JOB \& SSB, Table~\ref{tab:priority3_validation} Part~B):} When trained strictly on the 20 development seeds (\texttt{S1}--\texttt{S4}, $N_{\text{dev}}=60$ queries) and evaluated on the 5 locked unseen test seeds (\texttt{L1\_\allowbreak{}S5}--\texttt{L5\_\allowbreak{}S5}, $N_{\text{unseen}}=15$ queries), AST-Route ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$) achieves a $73.33\%$ Top-1 routing accuracy ($11/15$ queries, $86.67\%$ Top-2 [$13/15$]), a pure engine execution latency of $47.01\text{ ms}$ ($51.16\text{ ms}$ net end-to-end including $4.15\text{ ms}$ router dispatch, approaching the Oracle lower bound of $41.30\text{ ms}$), and a 95th-percentile tail latency of $79.64\text{ ms}$---matching the exact P95 tail latency of the Oracle Ideal Router ($79.64\text{ ms}$). On these unseen seeds, AST-Route outperforms Always DuckDB ($210.55\text{ ms}$, $385.97\%$ regret) by $4.48\times$, Always Polars ($81.49\text{ ms}$, $164.66\%$ regret) by $1.73\times$, Always GPU ($54.78\text{ ms}$, $118.57\%$ regret) by $1.17\times$, and Rule-Based Routing ($64.17\text{ ms}$, P95: $168.59\text{ ms}$) by $1.37\times$ while cutting P95 tail latency by $52.8\%$. Furthermore, to test strict zero-shot cross-schema transfer without retraining or hyperparameter tuning, we locked the $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ model trained exclusively on the 75 TPC-H queries and evaluated it across two external multi-join benchmarks (Table~\ref{tab:priority3_validation} Part~B): (i) \emph{all $33/33$ ($100\%$) canonical join-order template families} (\texttt{1a}--\texttt{33a}, 4-way to 10-way cyclic/snowflake joins over 21 tables, mean $J=7.00$, mean $\text{CSCI}=30.50$; selecting the primary \texttt{a}-variant per template from the 113-query suite so all 33 join graphs are represented equally without intra-template leakage across variants \texttt{b/c/d}) of the Join Order Benchmark (JOB)~\cite{leis2015job} on the real-world IMDB dataset ($\sim 3.6\text{ GB}$), and (ii) all 13 standard star-join queries (\texttt{Q1.1}--\texttt{Q4.3} across Flights 1--4, 1-way to 5-way joins over \texttt{lineorder}, \texttt{customer}, \texttt{supplier}, \texttt{part}, and \texttt{date}, mean $J=3.15$, mean $\text{CSCI}=24.19$) of the Star Schema Benchmark (SSB)~\cite{oneil2009ssb} at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ ($6.0\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineorder} rows, translated deterministically across engines via \texttt{SQLGlot}). Using individual queries as the unit of analysis for 10,000-iteration paired bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals and two-sided paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Holm--Bonferroni step-down correction across multiple engine comparisons, we observe distinct, complementary generalization behaviors across the two external schemas. On JOB ($N=33$), raw point-estimate $\arg\min$ routes $17/33$ ($51.52\%$) queries directly to Polars and $16/33$ ($48.48\%$) to GPU; however, the uncertainty-aware dispatcher abstains on all $16/33$ ($\mathbf{48.48\%}$ total abstention rate) GPU-predicted queries---comprising $7/33$ ($\mathbf{21.21\%}$) via Rule~U1's $12\%$ margin guard and $9/33$ ($\mathbf{27.27\%}$) via Rule~U3's unanchored \texttt{LIKE} string guard---thereby selecting Polars across all $17/33 + 16/33 = \mathbf{33/33\ (100\%)}$ templates and yielding an execution profile that matches Always Polars ($6.75\text{ ms}$ mean, $6.52\text{ ms}$ median, $10.51\text{ ms}$ P95, paired difference $\Delta T = 0.00\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[0.00, 0.00]\text{ ms}$, $p=1.000$). We emphasize that AST-Route does not outperform Always Polars on JOB; rather, its value lies in autonomously identifying Polars as the safe, low-variance choice without prior IMDB profiling. Because Cold GPU ($9.05\text{ ms}$ mean, $4.45\text{ ms}$ median, $29.26\text{ ms}$ P95, $124.57\%$ mean regret) is the true Top-1 Oracle on $26/33$ in-VRAM join queries (where Polars ranks second within $1.4\text{--}2.3\text{ ms}$), selecting Polars yields a $21.21\%$ Top-1 accuracy ($7/33$ queries), a $100.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy ($33/33$ queries), a $21.21\%$ near-optimal ($\le 5\%$) hit rate, a $30.30\%$ near-optimal ($\le 10\%$) hit rate ($10/33$ queries), a $43.84\%$ median regret, an $84.49\%$ mean regret ($181.2\%$ P95 regret), and a small absolute latency gap of $+2.72\text{ ms}$ relative to the $4.03\text{ ms}$ Oracle. Crucially, on the remaining $7/33$ highly selective string-filtered queries (\texttt{LIKE '\%...\%'}), avoiding GPU offloading prevents severe $19.8\text{--}26.1\text{ ms}$ PCIe transfer stalls, enabling AST-Route to statistically outperform Always GPU by $1.34\times$ (query-level paired $\Delta T = -2.30\text{ ms}$, 10,000-bootstrap 95\% CI: $[-3.42, -1.21]\text{ ms}$, two-sided Wilcoxon $p=0.0398$), Always DuckDB ($19.55\text{ ms}$, $2.90\times$, paired $\Delta T = -12.80\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[-14.85, -10.74]\text{ ms}$, $p < 0.001$, Holm--Bonferroni $p_{\text{Holm}}=6.98\times 10^{-10}$), and Always DataFusion ($31.25\text{ ms}$, $4.63\times$, $p_{\text{Holm}}=6.98\times 10^{-10}$). Conversely, on the external SSB star-schema workload ($N=13$ queries, \texttt{Q1.1}--\texttt{Q4.3}) where numeric and date range predicates align with zone-map selectivity $\sigma(q)$, zero-shot AST-Route dynamically dispatches Flight 1 and selective Flight 2/3 queries (\texttt{Q1.1}--\texttt{Q1.3}, \texttt{Q2.2}--\texttt{Q2.3}, \texttt{Q3.3}--\texttt{Q3.4}) to Polars Arrow and compute-heavy 3-to-5-way star joins (\texttt{Q2.1}, \texttt{Q3.1}--\texttt{Q3.2}, \texttt{Q4.1}--\texttt{Q4.3}) to the GPU, achieving $92.31\%$ Top-1 accuracy ($12/13$ queries), $100.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy ($13/13$ queries), $92.31\%$ near-optimal ($\le 5\%$ and $\le 10\%$) hit rates, a $12.15\text{ ms}$ mean latency ($11.85\text{ ms}$ median, $18.79\text{ ms}$ P95 vs.\ $11.75\text{ ms}$ Oracle, $+0.40\text{ ms}$ absolute latency gap), $0.00\%$ median regret, and $2.82\%$ mean regret---statistically outperforming Always Polars ($13.75\text{ ms}$, $1.13\times$), Always GPU ($13.91\text{ ms}$, $1.14\times$, paired $\Delta T = -1.76\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[-3.18, -0.42]\text{ ms}$), and Always DuckDB ($21.17\text{ ms}$, $1.74\times$, paired $\Delta T = -9.02\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[-11.60, -6.58]\text{ ms}$, Wilcoxon $p=0.00024$). Because JOB ($N=33$) and SSB ($N=13$) represent finite benchmark suites, these results demonstrate bounded cross-schema generalization across star and snowflake analytical topologies rather than universal optimality across arbitrary production workloads.

\caption{Out-of-sample generalization and cost-model validation: (a) Routing latency and regret on the 5 locked unseen holdout seeds (\texttt{L1\_\allowbreak{}S5}--\texttt{L5\_\allowbreak{}S5}, $N=15$ queries trained strictly on 20 development seeds \texttt{S1}--\texttt{S4}); (b) Tier-by-tier latency breakdown comparing Rule-Based Routing against Proposed \textbf{AST-Route} and the Oracle across L1--L5; and (c) Multiplicative Q-Error (Median P50 and 90th percentile P90) of the multi-output cost and resource predictor.}
\label{fig:priority3_unseen_cost}

\textbf{Mechanistic 12D Skew, String-Selectivity, and Join-Cardinality Ablation on JOB ($\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{skew}} \in \mathbb{R}^{12}$).} To test whether JOB's $21.21\%$ Top-1 ceiling under $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$ stems specifically from min/max zone-map blindness to substring filters and Zipfian foreign-key skew, we augmented $\mathbf{x}_q$ with three strictly pre-execution catalog features extracted in $9.50\,\mu\text{s}$ median lookup time from compact ($10.18\text{ KB}$) table sketches without scanning data pages: (i) trigram and Most-Common-Value (MCV) string-predicate selectivity $\hat{\sigma}_{\text{str}}(q)$, (ii) heavy-hitter join-key skew concentration $\hat{\zeta}_{\text{skew}}(q) \in [0,1]$, and (iii) log-scaled skew-adjusted Selinger join-cardinality fanout $\hat{\psi}_{\text{join}}(q)$. Under post-hoc exploratory catalog-sketch transfer (applying a TPC-H-calibrated fanout crossover gate over the locked TPC-H top-2 candidate set with zero IMDB runtime labels after diagnosing 9D string blindness), $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{skew}} \in \mathbb{R}^{12}$ retains narrow string-filtered queries on Polars to avoid $19.8\text{--}26.1\text{ ms}$ PCIe stalls while offloading dense multi-way joins (17 Polars / 16 GPU), elevating exploratory JOB Top-1 accuracy from $21.21\%$ to $69.70\%$ ($100.00\%$ Top-2), lowering mean latency from $6.75\text{ ms}$ to $4.54\text{ ms}$ ($\Delta T = -2.22\text{ ms}$, $95\%$ CI: $[-3.21, -1.31]\text{ ms}$), and cutting mean regret from $84.49\%$ to $16.50\%$ (median regret $0.00\%$; paired $\Delta\mathcal{R} = -67.99\%$, $95\%$ CI: $[-98.26\%, -40.30\%]$, Wilcoxon $p = 0.000211$). Furthermore, stepwise 5-fold template-disjoint calibration on IMDB reduces mean regret progressively from $84.49\%$ ($9\text{D}$) $\rightarrow 4.05\%$ ($10\text{D}$: $+\hat{\sigma}_{\text{str}}$) $\rightarrow 3.55\%$ ($11\text{D}$: $+\hat{\zeta}_{\text{skew}}$) $\rightarrow 3.55\%$ ($12\text{D}$, 8 Polars / 25 GPU, reaching $96.97\%$ Top-1 and $100.00\%$ Top-2 at $4.14\text{ ms}$ mean latency vs.\ $4.03\text{ ms}$ Oracle, $0.00\%$ median regret, $\Delta\mathcal{R} = -80.95\%$, $p < 0.001$), confirming that compact string/skew catalog sketches directly resolve the PCIe-vs.-join discrimination bottleneck. Table~\ref{tab:jbd_job_regimes_t11b} and Fig.~\ref{fig:job_12d_ablation_fig} synthesize the complete zero-shot and stepwise 5-fold template-disjoint progression on IMDB JOB ($N=33$ templates), contrasting static single-engine policies against 9D conformal abstention and compact 10.18~KB pre-execution catalog sketches.

\par\noindent{\scriptsize \textbf{Four-Point Zero-Leakage Audit:} (1) \emph{Static Base-Table Sketch Construction:} All three sketch coordinates ($\hat{\sigma}_{\text{str}}, \hat{\zeta}_{\text{skew}}, \hat{\psi}_{\text{join}}$; $10.18\text{ KB}$ total, $9.50\ \mu\text{s}$ lookup) are pre-computed once from static IMDB base tables (analogous to \texttt{ANALYZE} catalog statistics) with \textbf{zero access to test-fold runtime labels}; (2) \emph{Label-Free Levels 1 (Strict Zero-Shot 9D) \& 2 (Post-Hoc Exploratory 12D):} Use $0/33$ JOB execution labels, locking all weights and thresholds on TPC-H; (3) \emph{Template-Disjoint Outer Split:} Evaluates exactly one representative query per canonical template (\texttt{1a--33a}, $0\%$ template leakage across folds); and (4) \emph{Inner-Fold Hyperparameter \& Threshold Selection:} In Level 3 (explicitly designated as Supervised In-Domain Calibration rather than Zero-Shot), all GBDT parameters and fanout thresholds are tuned via Inner 4-Fold CV strictly inside the $26\text{--}27$ training templates of each outer fold without touching the held-out test fold.}

\end{figure}


\item \textbf{Regret Quantile Distribution and Tier-by-Tier Comparison Against Rule-Based Heuristics (Table~\ref{tab:priority3_validation}, Part~C):} Across the full 75-query workload, \textbf{AST-Route} achieves a median (P50) regret of $\mathbf{0.00\%}$ ($58.67\%$ zero-regret exact hits) and compresses 90th-percentile (P90) tail regret to $\mathbf{89.75\%}$, compared to $215.57\%$ for Rule-Based Routing ($2.40\times$ tail regret reduction) and $174.03\%$ for Always GPU. Tier-by-tier analysis (Fig.~\ref{fig:priority3_unseen_cost}b) reveals why learned cost regression outperforms static heuristics: while Rule-Based Routing matches \textbf{AST-Route} in L4 window functions ($19.37\text{ ms}, 100.0\%$ accuracy), static rules fail severely in L5 array unnesting ($145.62\text{ ms}, 225.86\%$ regret, $0.0\%$ Top-1 accuracy) by routing full-table $6.00\times 10^6$-row \texttt{UNNEST} queries to CPU Arrow engines. By contrast, \textbf{AST-Route} predicts that SIMT GPU radix expansion amortizes PCIe transfer costs in L5, reducing mean latency to $\mathbf{51.51\text{ ms}}$ ($23.57\%$ regret, $\mathbf{2.83\times}$ faster than Rule-Based Routing).
\end{enumerate}


\subsection{Multi-Scale Big Data Evaluation ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}$), Memory/VRAM Walls, Cross-Platform, Concurrency, and Nested LOO-CV on Official TPC-H Q1--Q22}
Table~\ref{tab:jbd_multiscale_t12} and Additional File~1 (Table~S10) and Fig.~\ref{fig:bigdata_multiscale} report the multi-scale Big Data experiments across the four standard TPC-H scale factors ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, \text{and } 100\text{ GB}$) \cite{tpc2022tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}, three hardware platform profiles, multi-tenant concurrent streams ($C=1\text{--}32$), and the 22 official TPC-H benchmark queries (Q1--Q22):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Unified Four-Scale Memory-Hierarchy Transitions and Out-of-Core Disk Spilling ($\text{SF}=1 \rightarrow 10 \rightarrow 30 \rightarrow 100\text{ GB}$, Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part A):} Scaling the full 75-query workload baseline across two orders of magnitude exposes three distinct hardware regimes (Fig.~\ref{fig:bigdata_multiscale}a--b): (i) at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ ($6.0\text{M}$ \texttt{lineitem} rows), working sets fit entirely in memory ($4.71\text{ GB}$ RAM, $0.0\text{ MB}$ disk spill, $1.30\text{ GB}$ VRAM, $1\times$ PCIe transfer), with \textbf{AST-Route} achieving $\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ ($3.35\times$ faster than DuckDB at $183.74\text{ ms}$ and $1.50\times$ faster than Polars at $82.47\text{ ms}$); (ii) at $\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$ ($60.0\text{M}$ rows), data exceeds the $4.0\text{ GB}$ GPU VRAM ceiling, requiring $4\times$ chunked PCIe streaming ($3.45\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM, $195.03\text{ ms}$ on GPU vs. $950.88\text{ ms}$ on DuckDB and $656.75\text{ ms}$ on Polars) while host RAM reaches $7.85\text{ GB}$ with minor spilling ($125.0\text{ MB}$), where \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{182.40\text{ ms}}$ ($\mathbf{5.21\times}$ speedup over DuckDB); (iii) at $\text{SF}=30\text{ GB}$ ($180.0\text{M}$ rows), local engines hit the $9.00\text{ GB}$ DBMS memory cap, triggering active out-of-core disk spilling \cite{kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} ($864.0\text{ MB}$ mean spill, $2594.49\text{ ms}$ on DuckDB, $1877.63\text{ ms}$ on Polars) and $12\times$ PCIe chunking on the GPU ($3.68\text{ GB}$ VRAM, $481.85\text{ ms}$), where \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{448.65\text{ ms}}$ ($\mathbf{5.78\times}$ speedup over DuckDB); and (iv) at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($600.1\text{M}$ \texttt{lineitem} rows, $866.1\text{M}$ total rows), extreme disk spilling ($3302.0\text{ MB}$ mean, $6840.0\text{ MB}$ in L2) slows local CPU engines to $6551.42\text{--}8699.98\text{ ms}$ ($256.20\text{--}385.40\text{ J}$). At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, Google BigQuery Serverless Cloud ($2571.60\text{ ms}$ server job mean across all five tiers---specifically $1493.51\text{ ms}$ in L1, $2302.18\text{ ms}$ in L2, $1700.85\text{ ms}$ in L3, $1286.16\text{ ms}$ in L4, and $6075.30\text{ ms}$ in L5, including $112.40\text{ ms}$ slot queueing delay; or $3356.20\text{ ms}$ total client wall-clock with $784.60\text{ ms}$ WAN HTTPS/gRPC result fetch) overtakes all local CPU engines by $\mathbf{2.55\times\text{--}3.38\times}$ ($1.95\times\text{--}2.59\times$ under full client WAN wall-clock) via horizontal slot elasticity, while \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{1142.80\text{ ms}}$ overall including router dispatch ($4.145\text{ ms}$) and $40\times$ chunked PCIe transfers ($\mathbf{7.61\times}$ faster than DuckDB and $\mathbf{5.73\times}$ faster than Polars).
    \item \textbf{Cross-Platform Hardware Generalization and Multi-Tenant Concurrency (Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part~B \& Additional File~1 (Table~S10) Part~C):} Under single-host resource-constrained thread/memory emulation (\textbf{Platform A}: 2 threads, $4\text{ GB}$ RAM) and mid-range nodes (\textbf{Platform B}: 4 threads, $8\text{ GB}$ RAM), \textbf{AST-Route} dispatches queries between DuckDB and Polars Arrow to achieve a $1.44\times$ scaling speedup ($234.50 \rightarrow 162.44\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$), while full workstation + GPU + Cloud deployment (\textbf{Platform C}: 8 threads, $16\text{ GB}$ RAM, RTX 2050 GPU) achieves a $\mathbf{5.49\times}$ mean speedup over DuckDB across all four scales ($54.92\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ to $1142.80\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$). Furthermore, under multi-tenant concurrent query streams ($C=1, 4, 8, 16,$ and $32$ concurrent queries in Additional File~1 (Table~S10) Part~C, explicitly separating active execution time from admission queuing delay), single-engine CPU execution suffers from memory-bus contention ($85.62 \rightarrow 228.58\text{ ms}$ at $C=16$ and $442.80\text{ ms}$ at $C=32$, plateauing at $70.00\text{--}72.27\text{ QPS}$), whereas AST-Route asynchronously dispatches scan/join streams across CPU cores ($94.1\%\text{--}98.2\%$ util) and GPU SIMT queues ($86.8\%\text{--}93.5\%$ util) simultaneously---cutting batch latency to $114.30\text{ ms}$ ($68.10\text{ ms}$ active execution + $46.20\text{ ms}$ queuing, $139.99\text{ QPS}$, $2.00\times$ gain) at $C=16$ and $207.20\text{ ms}$ ($82.40\text{ ms}$ execution + $124.80\text{ ms}$ queuing, P95: $268.5\text{ ms}$, P99: $312.6\text{ ms}$, $154.44\text{ QPS}$, $2.14\times$ gain) at $C=32$ with $0.0\%$ failure rate and $96.0\%\text{--}100.0\%$ routing stability (compressing P50/P90/P95/P99 tail latencies at $C=32$ from $395.0/612.4/698.5/842.0\text{ ms}$ on static CPU to $178.5/244.1/268.5/312.6\text{ ms}$ on AST-Route).
    \item \textbf{Strict Nested 22-Fold LOO-CV on Official Standard TPC-H Queries (Q1--Q22, Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part C):} On the 22 official TPC-H queries ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, mean $\text{CSCI}=26.52$) \cite{tpc2022tpch, boncz2014tpch}, DuckDB ($22.82\text{ ms}$, $0.709\text{ J}$) outperforms Polars ($42.79\text{ ms}$, $1.648\text{ J}$) by $1.88\times$ and DataFusion ($80.41\text{ ms}$, $2.895\text{ J}$) by $3.52\times$ due to mature correlated subquery unnesting in Q16--Q22, whereas CUDA GPU execution ($27.71\text{ ms}$ cold, $17.57\text{ ms}$ warm, $0.346\text{ J}$) dominates multi-way join choke-point queries Q1--Q15 ($4.06\text{--}12.78\text{ ms}$, including 5-way to 8-way join graphs in Q2, Q5, Q7, Q8, and Q9 that mirror complex join-order topologies). Under unweighted raw syntax LOO-CV, \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $27.33\text{ ms}$ ($40.91\%$ Top-1 accuracy, outperforming Always GPU, Always Polars by $1.57\times$, and Always DataFusion by $2.94\times$). Crucially, under \textbf{strict nested 22-fold LOO-CV} (where subquery-nesting feature weights are tuned strictly inside the 21-query inner training loop with zero information leakage from the held-out test query), \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{68.18\%}$ Top-1 accuracy, $\mathbf{90.91\%}$ Top-2 accuracy ($20/22$ queries), $\mathbf{81.82\%}$ near-optimal hit rate, and $\mathbf{16.98\text{ ms}}$ mean latency ($27.12\%$ mean regret, $0.435\text{ J}$ energy)---outperforming Always DuckDB by $\mathbf{1.34\times}$, Always GPU by $\mathbf{1.63\times}$, and Always Polars by $\mathbf{2.52\times}$, and closely approaching the post-hoc domain-calibrated bound ($15.84\text{ ms}, 72.73\%$ Top-1).
\item \textbf{Interconnect Crossover Adaptation, Bursty Contention Replay, and Fault-Tolerant Recovery:} To verify operational resilience beyond steady-state benchmarks under controlled hardware execution, we conducted three targeted production stress tests. First, contrasting 48-slice unpinned row-group staging ($3.36\text{ GB/s}$ effective H2D) against contiguous pinned PCIe Gen4 DMA ($5.69\text{ GB/s}$) across projected Scan, Star-Join, and Multi-Window workloads shifts L4 window $\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$ from $51.1\%$ (break-even $S_{\text{warm}}^* = 2.04\times$, $52.56\text{ ms}$ cold GPU vs.\ $77.29\text{ ms}$ Polars) down to $38.1\%$ ($S_{\text{warm}}^* = 1.62\times$, $41.82\text{ ms}$ cold GPU), with AST-Route's analytical break-even guard maintaining $100.0\%$ regime alignment alongside our $16$-core CPU-only node validation ($86.67\%$ Top-1). Second, replaying a $60$-query mixed ad-hoc stream (Scan, Join, CTE, Window, and UNNEST) under bursty arrivals ($4\text{ ms}$ inter-arrival spikes) and active background CPU/GPU contention ($\tau_{\text{SLA}}=250\text{ ms}$), AST-Route compresses mean queueing delay to $43.53\text{ ms}$ and mean end-to-end response time to $115.63\text{ ms}$ (P95: $244.71\text{ ms} < 250\text{ ms}$, $50.94\text{ QPS}$), cutting SLA violations to $3.33\%$ versus $78.33\%$ on Always Polars ($530.71\text{ ms}$), $76.67\%$ on Always DuckDB ($601.38\text{ ms}$), and $88.33\%$ on Always GPU ($973.84\text{ ms}$, $887.82\text{ ms}$ stream queue). Third, under live fault injection ($15$ real trials/scenario), proactive VRAM checks (\texttt{mem\_\allowbreak{}get\_\allowbreak{}info}) reroute VRAM-constrained queries in $0.155\text{ ms}$ ($12.64\text{ ms}$ total with Polars execution), process-capped mid-flight \texttt{OutOfMemoryError} catching ($15/15$ exceptions) recovers onto Polars in $1.05\text{ ms}$ ($12.50\text{ ms}$ total fallback latency), $50\text{ ms}$ cloud timeout circuit-breakers recover in $77.86\text{ ms}$, missing catalog footers fall back to parametric priors in $0.574\text{ ms}$ ($11.51\text{ ms}$ total), and offline primary engines reroute to Top-2 DuckDB in $11.66\text{ ms}$, achieving $0.00\%$ unhandled failures.
\end{enumerate}

At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, BigQuery ($2571.60\text{ ms}$ exact 5-tier mean across $N=75$: $1493.51\text{ ms}$ in L1, $2302.18\text{ ms}$ in L2, $1700.85\text{ ms}$ in L3, $1286.16\text{ ms}$ in L4, and $6075.30\text{ ms}$ in L5; decomposed into $112.40\text{ ms}$ slot queueing $+ 2459.20\text{ ms}$ distributed slot execution, plus $+784.60\text{ ms}$ client WAN HTTPS/gRPC fetch $= 3356.20\text{ ms}$ client wall-clock in Additional File~1 (Table~S15)) overtakes all local CPU engines ($6551.42\text{--}8699.98\text{ ms}$) by $\mathbf{2.55\times\text{--}3.38\times}$.}

% === BEGIN V9 SECTION 3.2 MULTI-SCALE SF=100 GB CREDIBILITY & BOUNDARY AUDIT ===
\subsubsection{Validation of Multi-Scale \texorpdfstring{$\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$}{SF=100 GB} Execution Boundaries: Cloud Queueing, Parquet Pruning Parity, Uniform Spill Governance, and Tier Reconciliation}
\label{sec:v9_sec3_2_multiscale_sf100_audit}
To establish rigorous execution boundaries for the multi-scale $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ results in Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch}, Additional File~1 (Table~S15) reports five cross-engine boundary verifications:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{BigQuery Cloud Fairness Protocol (BQ-1 to BQ-5: Cold vs.\ Warm Cache, Concurrency, Queue/Execution/WAN Decomposition, Multi-Window CV, and Scan Cost Fidelity; Part~A):} To ensure rigorous cloud vs.\ local fairness, we report five explicit BigQuery telemetry controls (\texttt{v9\_sec3\_2\_bigquery\_cloud\_latency\_queue\_network\_decomposition.csv}): (\textbf{BQ-1: Cold vs.\ Warm Result Cache}) All primary cloud benchmarks enforce cold execution (\texttt{QueryJobConfig(use\_query\_cache=False)}: $745.39\text{ ms}$ clustered vs.\ $1{,}412.60\text{ ms}$ unclustered at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$), whereas enabling the warm result cache (\texttt{use\_query\_cache=True}) reduces server latency to $148.20\text{ ms}$ ($28.40\text{ ms}$ queue $+ 119.80\text{ ms}$ cache lookup; $512.40\text{ ms}$ client WAN, $\$0.00$ billed); (\textbf{BQ-2: Multi-Tenant Concurrency Scaling}) Under $C \in \{1, 8, 32\}$ concurrent cloud streams, multi-tenant slot queueing scales smoothly from $\tau_{\text{queue}} = 48.20\rightarrow 62.40\rightarrow 94.60\text{ ms}$ ($745.39\rightarrow 798.50\rightarrow 912.40\text{ ms}$ server job) at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ and from $112.40\rightarrow 184.50\text{ ms}$ ($2{,}571.60\rightarrow 2{,}895.40\text{ ms}$) at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$; (\textbf{BQ-3: Three-Stage Queue, Server Execution, and Client WAN Decomposition Across $\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$}) Every BigQuery latency in Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} represents complete server job duration $T_{\text{BQ,server\_job}} = \tau_{\text{queue}} + \tau_{\text{server\_exec}}$, decomposed alongside client HTTPS/gRPC Arrow fetch ($\tau_{\text{client\_net}}$) across $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ ($48.20 + 697.19 = 745.39\text{ ms}$ server; $+379.40 = 1{,}124.79\text{ ms}$ client WAN), $\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$ ($64.50 + 1{,}187.75 = 1{,}252.25\text{ ms}$ server; $+485.20 = 1{,}737.45\text{ ms}$ client), $\text{SF}=30\text{ GB}$ ($86.10 + 1{,}702.84 = 1{,}788.94\text{ ms}$ server; $+612.80 = 2{,}401.74\text{ ms}$ client), and $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($112.40\text{ ms}$ [$4.37\%$] queue $+ 2{,}459.20\text{ ms}$ [$95.63\%$, $48{,}420\text{ totalSlotMs}$] exec $= 2{,}571.60\text{ ms}$ server; $+784.60\text{ ms}$ client WAN $= 3{,}356.20\text{ ms}$, still beating spill-bound local CPUs at $6{,}551.42\text{--}8{,}699.98\text{ ms}$ by $\mathbf{1.95\times\text{--}2.59\times}$); (\textbf{BQ-4: Multi-Window Repetition Stability}) Repeating $R=5$ timed trials across morning, afternoon, and night UTC windows yields a cross-window coefficient of variation of $\text{CV}=9.84\%$ ($731.20\text{--}761.80\text{ ms}$ window means); and (\textbf{BQ-5: Pre-Execution Clustered Scan Cost Fidelity}) Partition/clustering metadata predicts actual \texttt{totalBytesBilled} within $\text{MAE}=4.26\%$ and median Q-Error $=1.041$ (P90 $=1.118$, reducing on-demand scan fees from $\$1{,}267.66$ unclustered to $\$155.14/1\text{M}$ clustered at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$).
    \item \textbf{Identical Parquet Column Projection, Host Predicate Pushdown, and Exact PCIe Bandwidth Reconciliation Across CPU and GPU (Part~B):} The PyTorch CUDA backend does \emph{not} scan pre-filtered in-memory tensors or bypass storage I/O; rather, before any PCIe transfer, it invokes PyArrow's native C++ dataset scanner (\texttt{to\_batches(columns=projected\_cols, filter=pushdown\_expr)}) over the exact same physical ZSTD Parquet files on NVMe using the identical \texttt{SQLGlot}-extracted column list and row-group \texttt{min}/\texttt{max} zone-map predicate pushdown as DuckDB, Polars, and DataFusion. At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($27.20\text{ GB}$ total ZSTD catalog), all four local engines read the exact same $5241.0\text{ MB}$ ($19.27\%$) of projected/pruned Parquet bytes from NVMe. While the unfiltered full-table 3-column FP64 capacity ceiling at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ is $14{,}280.0\text{ MB}$ ($14.28\text{ GB}$, which defines the worst-case VRAM streaming loop bound $K_{\max}=40 \times 357.0\text{ MB}$ capacity slices so that peak device memory never exceeds $3.85\text{ GB} \le 4.00\text{ GB}$ VRAM), PyArrow C++ row-group pruning and predicate filter pushdown ($\bar{\sigma}=0.348$ across horizons V1/V2/V3) together with dictionary/INT32 key packing on the host CPU prior to H2D DMA reduce the \emph{workload-mean physical payload actually transferred over pinned PCIe Gen4 $\times 4$ across the 75 queries} to $\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\text{PCIe}} = 3{,}518.98\text{ MB}$ ($3.44\text{ GB}$, averaging $9.86$ active $357.0\text{ MB}$ slices or $87.97\text{ MB}$ per nominal slice). Dividing this post-pushdown payload by our measured sustained pinned PCIe Gen4 $\times 4$ DMA throughput ($5.69\text{ GB/s} = 5{,}690.0\text{ MB/s}$) yields $\tau_{\text{PCIe}} = 3{,}518.98\text{ MB} / 5{,}690.0\text{ MB/s} = \mathbf{618.45\text{ ms}}$ of physical PCIe H2D/D2H transfer plus $581.15\text{ ms}$ of non-overlapped host filtering/staging and CUDA kernel execution, matching the reported $\mathbf{1{,}199.60\text{ ms}}$ total GPU mean exactly (whereas transferring the full unpruned $14{,}280.0\text{ MB}$ ceiling synchronously at $5.69\text{ GB/s}$ without host pushdown would require $14{,}280.0 / 5{,}690.0 = 2{,}509.67\text{ ms}$ of PCIe transfer and $3{,}090.82\text{ ms}$ total, or $1{,}842.10\text{ ms}$ with 2-stream \texttt{cudaMemcpyAsync} compute/DMA overlap).
    \item \textbf{Uniform $9.00\text{ GB}$ RAM Cap and NVMe Disk Spill Governance Across Local Engines (Part~B):} All local CPU engines are constrained to the identical $M_{\text{host}} \le 9.00\text{ GB}$ working-set memory ceiling on the $16.0\text{ GB}$ host and spill intermediate hash/sort partitions to the same dedicated NVMe directory (\texttt{E:/nvme\_spill\_pool}), cleared and \texttt{fsync}-flushed prior to each trial. At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, DuckDB writes $3302.0\text{ MB}$ of mean NVMe spill ($2845.20\text{ ms}$ I/O stall), Polars streaming writes $2845.0\text{ MB}$ ($2190.40\text{ ms}$ stall), and DataFusion \texttt{FairSpillPool(9GB)} writes $3118.0\text{ MB}$ ($2468.50\text{ ms}$ stall), whereas the GPU's $40\times$ chunked streaming pipeline maintains compact summary accumulators in VRAM with $0.0\text{ MB}$ of disk spill.
    \item \textbf{Full Dispatch and Data Transfer Inclusion in AST-Route Across All Protocols (Part~C):} Every reported AST-Route latency at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ includes the full pre-execution router dispatch overhead ($T_{\text{router}} = 3.476\text{ ms}$ AST parse $+ 0.172\text{ ms}$ cached zone-map lookup $+ 0.497\text{ ms}$ GBR inference $= 4.145\text{ ms}$ warm, or $4.386\text{ ms}$ under cold NVMe \texttt{seek(-8)} footer reads) plus all PCIe H2D/D2H chunk transfers and cloud queueing delays: $1142.80\text{ ms}$ under Same-Scale 10D LOSO-CV ($4.15\text{ ms}$ router $+ 494.76\text{ ms}$ PCIe $+ 22.48\text{ ms}$ cloud queue $+ 621.41\text{ ms}$ compute), $1299.72\text{ ms}$ when adding full client WAN HTTPS/gRPC transfer ($+156.92\text{ ms}$) on the $20\%$ cloud-routed queries, $1326.90\text{ ms}$ under FinOps Zero-Cloud Policy B8 ($0\%$ cloud, $\$0.00$), $1186.40\text{ ms}$ under Cross-Scale 10D LOSO-CV, and $1214.50\text{ ms}$ under strict Leave-One-Scale-Out extrapolation.
    \item \textbf{Cloud vs.\ Local Storage Boundary and Exact L1--L5 Tier Closure (Part~A \& Part~D):} Under Boundary~1 (pre-staged warehouse), tables reside in native BigQuery Capacitor partitioned/clustered storage with query cache disabled. Under Boundary~2 (cold ad-hoc local-to-cloud ingestion), uploading the $27.20\text{ GB}$ ZSTD Parquet catalog to GCS and loading Capacitor requires $T_{\text{ingest}} \approx 840.0\text{ s}$, which amortizes against local spill-bound DuckDB after $N_{\text{BE}} = \lceil 840.0 / (8.700 - 2.572) \rceil = 138\text{ queries}$ ($158\text{ queries}$ under client WAN wall-clock, or $22\text{ queries}$ under concurrent L2/L4 spill contention). Finally, Part~D reconciles all five tiers ($15$ queries/tier, $N=75$) at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ with zero rounding discrepancy: BigQuery's latencies across L1 ($1493.51\text{ ms}$), L2 ($2302.18\text{ ms}$), L3 ($1700.85\text{ ms}$), L4 ($1286.16\text{ ms}$), and L5 ($6075.30\text{ ms}$) average to $\frac{1}{5}(1493.51+2302.18+1700.85+1286.16+6075.30) = \mathbf{2571.60\text{ ms}}$, while all other engines and spill volumes similarly close to $0.00\text{ ms}$ / $0.0\text{ MB}$ error against Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch}.
\end{enumerate}

\caption{Multi-scale Big Data evaluation across $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ ($6\text{M}$ rows), $\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$ ($60\text{M}$ rows), $\text{SF}=30\text{ GB}$ ($180\text{M}$ rows), and $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($600\text{M}$ rows): (a) Cross-engine execution latency scaling on a logarithmic scale; (b) Memory-hierarchy profiling illustrating the $4.0\text{ GB}$ GPU VRAM ceiling ($1\times, 4\times, 12\times, \text{and } 40\times$ PCIe chunks) and the $9.0\text{ GB}$ Host DBMS RAM cap with out-of-core disk spilling ($0 \rightarrow 125 \rightarrow 864 \rightarrow 3302\text{ MB}$); and (c) Cross-platform hardware scaling across 2-core, 4-core, and 8-core+GPU+Cloud profiles.}
